Each binding now also records its signed height along the baseline face normal, and the refit adds `height * (current normal - baseline normal)` so the standoff turns with the surface it is attached to. Only the normal component is transported: the tangential part of an edge- or corner-bound offset stays in the garment's own residual, so a hem that overhangs its driver cannot be swung around by a distant triangle, and binding a garment is still an exact no-op until a slider actually moves. Reset and Bake re-measure every binding's height against the geometry they rebase onto, so an already-baked rotation is not re-applied on the next slider move.
- **The release build failed at its last helper step whenever the .NET protocol gained a capability.** `build_pyside6_app.ps1` wrote the helper manifest from its own hand-copied transcript of `HelperBuildProvenance.RequiredProtocolCapabilities`, then started the published helper and compared the two — so `authoring_provisional_session_v1`, added on the C# side with the prewarm work, made the manifest 23 capabilities against the helper's 24 and stopped the build with ".NET Mesh Editor published provenance does not match its packaged manifest", after seventy-odd seconds of native and .NET compilation and with no indication of which field disagreed. The manifest now reads the capability list out of `HelperBuildProvenance.cs` and the version out of the `.csproj`, so the two can no longer drift, and a mismatch names every field that differs and lists the capabilities each side has that the other does not. The contract is also read in the first seconds of a build rather than only after the compile, so a declaration the script cannot parse stops it immediately.
- **A build blocked by the running app reported it as a permissions problem.** Publishing into `dist/` fails with "Access to the path ... is denied" when the portable exe is open, which reads as an ACL or antivirus fault and sends the reader looking in the wrong place — after five minutes of building, at the last step. The retry loops now name the processes running out of the path they could not replace, and say to close it.
- **The guard that was supposed to cover the helper manifest required the bug.** `test_release_packaging.py` asserted that nine named capabilities appear verbatim in `build_pyside6_app.ps1` — a check that can only pass while the duplication exists, and one carrying its own third copy of the list, three capabilities short. It was green throughout. It now asserts the derivation instead, and `tests/test_dotnet_helper_manifest_contract.py` runs the build script's own contract function against the .NET sources, in half a second, under the `mesh-unit` gate. Verified against a reshaped C# declaration and against a re-hardcoded list: it fails on both and names the fix.
- **"Solid (Textured)" was reported as succeeding without ever compiling a texture.** The resident helper launches with a deliberately textureless package — every submesh carries `"reason": "textures_on_demand"`, the manifest ships zero resources, and base falls back to `neutral_checker` — while the host seeds `standalone_dotnet_material_signature` from that same package and all three material generations start at zero. So on an unedited mesh the first textured request computed the very same signature, `generation <= completed` held as `0 <= 0`, and both the publish path and the resolve path took their "the helper already holds exactly these materials" shortcut. Nothing was compiled, nothing was sent, no acknowledgement was expected and no failure was logged — the viewport was simply told to show `textured` over a package with no material resources, so `MaterialHasBase` stayed 0 and Solid (Textured) drew exactly like Faces (No Textures), Solid + Wire exactly like Faces + Wire. Both shortcuts now also require that a material generation was actually applied, which is the condition the display-mode handler already used and these two paths did not.
- **X-Ray applied to one pane only.** `context.XRay` had exactly two writers — `TrySetDisplayMode` and `SaveActivePresentationContext` — and both touch the active pane's context alone; it was the one piece of display state missing from `SynchronizePresentationDisplaySettings`, the only fan-out to every context. Since each pane renders from its own context, picking X-Ray changed whichever pane happened to be active, the Original/reference one, and left the other still depth-testing its overlays. X-Ray is derived by the same switch that sets the display mode, so it now travels with it.
- **A host-driven display-mode change reached one pane; the helper's own combo reached all of them.** `viewport_display_update` — the route the Edit Mesh tool rail drives — called the bare `TrySetDisplayMode`, while the helper's internal Preview mode combo called `TrySetSynchronizedDisplayMode`. The host route uses the synchronized setter too now, so the two controls can no longer disagree about what the viewport is showing.
- **Mesh View's "Solid (Textured)" loaded no textures, and "Solid + Wire" would not stay selected.** Both symptoms were one abort. A normal or height map whose raw `.dds` is packaged verbatim for the resident viewport is deliberately never decoded to a preview PNG, because the combiner's own output for that channel is discarded anyway — but the combiner still walked the input, could not open a `.dds` through `QImageReader`, and recorded `normal unreadable:<name>.dds`. The material compiler treats any `unreadable:` note as a hard blocker, so the whole compile failed and no textures ever reached the viewport, for every mesh carrying a raw normal map. The controls were telling the truth about that: a textured mode that cannot be entered snaps back to its untextured fallback, which is why picking Solid + Wire left "Faces + Wire" showing. A skipped decode is now labelled as one, and only a genuine read failure still blocks.
- **The display-mode gate only ever drove four of the eight modes it ships.** `Faces + Wire`, `Solid + Wire`, `Wire` and `X-Ray` were never asked for, never acknowledged and never captured, so "Solid + Wire" could reach users broken behind a green harness run. All eight are exercised against a real `.pac` now, each checked against the viewport's own flag table and against draw counters that must advance, and the harness table is pinned to the combo table so a mode added to the UI fails the guard until it is covered.
- **Opening the Mesh Editor showed the editor assembling itself, then loading.** The embedded helper's window was revealed at the end of its startup realization — before the texture load it starts on the very next line, and therefore seconds before the first drawn frame. The reader got a fully built but empty editor: panels, rail and splitters painted by WinForms over a viewport with nothing in it, then the model arriving separately. That reveal could not simply be moved later, because the D3D11 device was created by the first paint, a hidden window never gets one, and `TryApplyMaterialState` fails outright without a device — so the reveal *had* to precede the textures for the textures to bind at all. The renderer is now brought up explicitly while the window is still hidden (`EnsureRendererInitialized`, which needs the handle, not visibility), and the reveal moves to the moment geometry, materials and textures are all bound. Measured against the real helper, the window used to appear ~3.5 s before `ready` and now appears 0.8 ms after `textures_ready`. It cannot wait for the frame itself — the frame is produced by a paint — so this is as late as it goes. Every terminal texture failure reveals too, or an error would be reported into a status line behind the host's spinner.
- **The Mesh Editor's helper could not be warmed until the moment it was needed.** The resident architecture was already there — one helper process, scenes hot-swapped into it, hidden rather than killed on exit — but the *first* open per edit session still paid a cold process start, JIT and D3D device creation, because the prewarm could only begin once the authoritative edit-session id existed. That ordering was load-bearing: an authoring handshake made before the session existed latched a throwaway id, and both sides then refused the real one (`session_mismatch` in the helper, "authoring session changed while the resident helper was active" in the host). A prewarm handshake is now marked provisional and may be adopted exactly once, by exactly one real session, before it has owned any mutation; a real session can still never be displaced. The alignment preview shell starts the helper 750 ms after it opens, so the start-up is spent while the user is still choosing what to edit. The rebind is gated on the helper advertising `authoring_provisional_session_v1` rather than required, so an older helper still runs — and a prewarm holding a session it cannot hand back is dropped rather than being allowed to refuse a real one.
- **The Mesh Editor's layout re-asserted itself on screen every time it opened.** The helper emits `activated` *after* it has revealed its window, and the host answered that by pushing session, scene and presentation state — including the interaction mode. A mesh-edit interaction frame makes the helper re-run `ApplyInteractionModeControls`, which re-asserts the tool rail and re-parents every section, so the reader watched the editor rearrange itself immediately after it appeared. The state was already correct: `_rehydrate_shared_dotnet_controller` sends exactly the same three frames from inside the package-apply path, which runs *before* activation and therefore while the window is still hidden. The `activated` handler now skips the push when the rehydrator has already delivered it for the current process generation — a suspend/resume, which activates without a package apply and so has no rehydrate behind it, still sends its state.
- **A prewarmed helper flashed its placeholder scene into the pane before the real model.** Moving the prewarm earlier exposed a stage that had been harmless while it only ever ran at click time: the helper revealed its window at the end of its own start-up regardless of *what* it was showing, and a prewarm is showing a procedural placeholder nobody asked to see. Measured on the wire, the window was revealed, painted, presented, and hidden again 67 ms later when the host answered `ready` with `deactivate_request` — a flash of the wrong model at dialog-open time, and a flicker the earlier reveal fix did not cover because it was never a startup-ordering problem. A prewarm launch is flagged (`--prewarm`) and stays hidden throughout; the host never needed `ready` from one, because it warms the GPU with an offscreen capture instead. The window is revealed by the `activate_request` that follows a real package, 15 ms after `package_load_applied` — by which point geometry, materials and textures are all bound, so the first frame the pane ever shows is the real model. With the process and device already warm, that whole path now takes 33 ms from request to revealed.
- **Every tool rail page in the Mesh Editor said its own name twice, and the Move page said two different names.** The tool dock header already names the page, so the section box beneath it repeated it verbatim — "SELECTION" over a "SELECTION" group, and the same for Brush, Topo, Colour and Morph. Worse on Move, where the header read "TRANSFORM" while the rail button, the tool it activates and the status line all say Move. The header is Move now, and the section a rail page is built around drops its own caption. Part Pick keeps its caption, because it is the second box on the Selection page, as do the scene groups on the right, which have no header of their own. The classic layout has no dock header, so it gets every caption back.
- **Dragging the Mesh Editor's placement Gizmo tore the window apart and left the mesh behind.** Every `placement_transform_request` — one per pointer sample, so roughly one every 30 ms — was answered with a full transform update: an authoritative scene frame recalculated over every vertex on a worker, published to the resident helper, and a static preview rebuild armed behind it. Each of those frames made the helper re-run `ApplyInteractionModeControls`, which in placement mode restores the classic layout: a redraw batch over the whole form, every tool section cleared and re-added, both flanks collapsed and re-revealed. At drag cadence that is the flicker, and it reads as the Edit Mesh layout because that restore is the same code path leaving Edit Mesh takes. Intermediate samples now only move the spin boxes and their sliders; the terminal sample publishes the frame that makes the placement authoritative again, and the helper re-asserts its interaction-mode controls only when the mode actually changed — the guard a package swap already used.
- **A gizmo drag stopped following the pointer once an authoritative frame landed mid-drag.** The drag renders from a provisional placement snapshot taken at mouse-down, and a frame whose placement matched cleared that snapshot. If the pointer happened to be at rest when one arrived — the common case, because the frame echoes a sample the pointer has already reached — the next drag sample re-took the snapshot, pairing the already-moved translation with the pre-move model matrix. From then on the mesh sat at the echoed sample and every further movement was measured from a stale base. A live drag owns its snapshot until it ends now, and a drag left open by a lost mouse-up is closed on the next pointer move rather than stalling every later frame.
- **Unticking Gizmo (or Grid) came undone on the next scene update.** Overlay visibility belongs to the two checkboxes and arrives through presentation updates, but every authoritative scene frame writes `"visible": true` for both and the helper adopted it — so switching an overlay off held until anything republished the scene, such as nudging a Location value. Both flags are host state now and survive an incoming frame, the same way the resident world grid's origin and spacing already did.
- **"Use This as Swap Source" did nothing on the v2 backend, which is the normal way the swap is used.** Prepared dependency snapshots are held in a four-slot LRU, and choosing a swap source means previewing other files — so arming a target and then browsing to find its source evicted the target's own snapshot within four previews. `archive_workflow_dependency_context` then raised, and `_start_archive_in_game_mesh_swap` reported it through `set_status_message`, which renders on the Textures tab: from Archive Browser the click did nothing and said nothing, the same shape as the arming bug one step earlier. The armed target's snapshot is pinned against eviction for as long as the swap is armed, including across the `clear_snapshot` a scope change performs, and both failure paths now raise a dialog naming the target, the source, and the reason. Found from a real session log that showed the target previewed, then four other files previewed, then a click that produced no event at all.
- **The swap banner now says when its target is not ready yet.** Arming can beat the asynchronous dependency preparation, and a pin recorded before the snapshot exists only takes effect once it lands — so there was a window where the banner promised a swap that would have failed. The pin is applied from the banner refresh, which keeps the two from ever disagreeing, and the banner reads "Still preparing this target's dependencies" until the snapshot arrives; the ready handler refreshes it so the message clears by itself.
- **An in-game mesh swap from a cloth-bearing donor crashed before the scope dialog appeared.** `contract_warning` was built inside the `source_has_larger_material_contract` branch and then used one level out, under `preserve_source_contract_default` — which is also true when the donor merely carries cloth/PBD. A weapon donor with a tassel or banner but no more material wrappers than its target therefore raised `UnboundLocalError` on the way to the dialog, so the swap did nothing and said nothing. The warning is built whenever the contract is detected now.
- **Starting an in-game mesh swap looked like it had done nothing.** Arming a target wrote its confirmation to `set_status_message`, which lands on the Textures tab's progress panel — not visible from Archive Browser — and otherwise only changed the label of a menu item inside the collapsed "Mesh Edit" dropdown. The only way to discover you had armed anything was to right-click the same file again and read "Cancel In-Game Mesh Swap Target". Archive Browser now carries a banner above the file list naming the target and offering one-click Cancel, and it survives filtering, because filtering to find the source is the next thing you do.
- **Arming a swap from the action button jumped to the Mesh Editor first.** `_swap_current_archive_mesh_with_in_game` opened the editor before setting the target, so the button moved you away from the browser you needed to pick the source in. Both the button and the Mesh Editor's own In-Game Swap now arm and stay put — or return to Archive Browser — and the editor opens once both sides are known, which also stops a redundant `mesh_edit` frame being pushed at the tool rail.
- **A donor's cloth/PBD contract was detected and then discarded.** Complete In-Game Swap regenerates the target material sidecar from the alignment mapping, and nothing in that rebuild carries `_pbdSimulationMaterialName` or `_physicsFileName` across — both are read for reporting only. The preflight already detected the donor contract, but `complete_swap_scope_default` was hardcoded to `True`, so the detection changed a warning label and nothing else. A donor with cloth or a wider material contract now defaults to Select Item Family, which copies the source sidecar onto the target sidecar path, and its physics rows are preselected even across weapon folders, because a copied sidecar names them.
- **Half the in-game swap scope table's explanations never rendered.** The dialog looked its relationship edges up by a `"pamt::path"` string while the preflight keyed them by `ArchiveEntryIdentity`, so every lookup missed: the "What this row means" column fell back to generic heuristics and the graph-derived default checks never applied. Both sides key on `ArchiveEntryIdentity` now, which also drops a `Path.resolve()` per row that the old string key needed.
- **The swap scope dialog now warns when a checked row has no target file.** Retargeting a source companion to a target that has no counterpart falls through to a string-substituted guess, and a payload written there is referenced by nothing the game loads. That is the normal case for physics when the target has none of its own, and it silently produced a mod that did nothing.
- **Unticking "Load textures" did nothing against a textured model.** `_archive_textures_visible` mirrors what the renderer is showing, and the two desync on the very first load: the package load asks for `untextured_wire` and records `False`, while the renderer settles on `textured` because the package carries texture resources. The hide branch was gated on that mirror being `True`, so the display mode was never sent at all — which is why fixing the renderer's package-swap default and the silent rejection did not help. It is stated on every toggle now rather than trusted from a local belief about what the viewport already shows.
- **The Archive Browser preview refused every display change the host sent it.** Loading a simple-preview package makes the renderer latch that package's scene session id — `archive_preview` — as its resident session, and it then compares every correlated message against it by ordinal equality. Adopting a package's session id was wired only for the authoring profile, because an edit session owns the helper and must not be stolen; the preview profile kept the random UUID it generates at construction, so `AcceptMaterialSession` refused the lot. Unticking "Load textures" sent a correct `untextured_wire` that was rejected on arrival, which is why fixing the host's decision path three times changed nothing. A preview owns nothing, so it now follows the package.
- **A submesh that shares a material with another submesh rendered as a flat tint instead of its texture.** A base texture derived by convention from the material's normal map is registered against the first wrapper that declared it, and base selection then dropped any candidate whose wrapper index was not the mesh's own local index. The support roles were given an exemption for this in the past — a binding naming the mesh's own material — and base never got one, so the second submesh kept its normal, surface and height maps and lost only its albedo. On `cd_phm_02_sword_0013`, `cd_phm_02_acc_0032` is bound to two accessory submeshes and only the first was textured; on `cd_m0001_00_geumgangbulgwe_nude_0001` the second head submesh was picking up a `cd_pgm_` texture from another asset family entirely.
- Sharing a material *name* is not enough to make two wrappers' textures interchangeable, so the exemption is narrower than the support-role one: the candidate's own texture family also has to be the mesh's material. `cd_m0041_00_woodenowl_00_0001` is why — its Eye and Body submeshes both declare `CD_M0041_00_WoodenOwl_0001` yet bind `blackoil` and the owl's own atlas, and the looser rule let the Eye's texture reach five other batches. Across a 40-asset sample, 105 of 107 batch selections are unchanged, two improve, none are lost.
- **A rejected display change was invisible in the Archive Browser.** Only the Mesh Editor read `presentation_state_update_ack`, so when the renderer refused an update — a stale session or process generation — the toolbar showed the new state over a viewport that had not changed, with no message anywhere. This is the shape of "unticking Load textures does nothing". The Archive Browser reports the refusal and its reason now.
- **A package swap put textures back after they had been turned off.** `ReplaceResidentPackage` settled on the package's own default mode, which is `textured` whenever the package carries texture resources, discarding whatever the host had last asked for. Once the host has named a display mode it owns the choice and a swap keeps it; a package with no textures still falls back to the untextured equivalent rather than a mode it cannot honour.
- **Changing the .NET/Vortice cache mode pruned only half the cache, and Clear Preview Cache cleared only half of it.** Durable preview packages live in two tiers under `preview/models`: `packages`, holding the decoded output, and `dotnet_vortice/packages`, holding the Vortice-ready packages the resident renderer actually loads. Both are bounded independently at store time, but prune and clear walked only the first, because each took a single cache root and appended `packages` to it. Dropping Aggressive to Balanced left the derived tier at its old size, and Clear Preview Cache reported that it had removed "durable .NET/Vortice preview packages" while leaving the tier named after them on disk. `prune`/`clear` now take every tier, and the derived root has one accessor rather than four hardcoded `"dotnet_vortice"` literals that could drift apart again.
- **Setting the cache to Off reclaimed nothing.** Off means a zero budget, and a zero budget makes the prune return before it looks at anything, so choosing "Off (least disk use)" left every byte an earlier mode had written. It clears the durable tiers now; packages pinned by a live preview are still skipped.
- **A queued preview prewarm could take the whole app down.** `_DotNetPreviewPrewarmTask` runs on the *global* thread pool and nothing waits for it, so it can outlive the host that started it. `self.signals` is then a deleted QObject and the emit raises `Internal C++ object already deleted` — and that emit sat outside the guard, so the exception escaped `QRunnable.run()`. An exception escaping a Python override of a C++ virtual is fatal: PySide6 reports `Error calling Python override of QRunnable::run()` and the process dies. In the app that means closing a preview while a prewarm is in flight; in CI it killed whichever test happened to be running when the task came due, which is how it presented as a crash in the mesh builder tests at 24%.
- A prewarm is best-effort by definition, so nothing in it is worth a crash: `run()` now contains its own failures and treats a departed host as nobody left to tell.

- **The Translation Studio tests built a whole tab per test and destroyed none of them**, so the widget trees accumulated across the class and the interpreter died part-way through that file at 98% of a full run, on 3.14 only, while 3.11 finished clean with 7,700 passing. The tabs are closed and their deletion processed now. This is the fourth instance of one pattern this evening, after the preview session controller, the placement studio window, and the archive backend client: a Qt object built with no parent and left for the collector, destroyed later inside an unrelated test's Qt work, aborting the process with no traceback and no summary. Widgets need `close()` then `deleteLater()` then a `processEvents()` pass; forcing the deferred delete instead destroys them mid-teardown and crashes.

- **A finished Mesh Editor action could leave a progress message as the last word on the status line.** Progress arrives on a queued cross-thread signal, so one emitted just before the result can be delivered just after it, and the handler only checked that the request id was current, never that the request had already finished. The status line then read `Applied Rotate.` where the completion handler had just written the session state, revision and undo depth. Each terminal handler records the request as finished now and progress for it is ignored. Found because it failed CI on one interpreter leg and not the other, which is exactly the shape a delivery-order race takes.

- **Opening Translation Studio took 3.6 seconds, and pressing Load paid the same 3.6 seconds again.** Both went to one question — which package holds this language? — answered by parsing all 33 package tables on the UI thread, inside the tab's constructor. Almost all of it was waste: the fourteen `.paloc` tables live in packages 0019–0032, which hold **one entry each** and parse in under a millisecond, while the time goes to 0000, 0004 and 0009, which carry 200,000–400,000 entries apiece and not one localization string between them.
- Nothing can know that without parsing, so the new `language_index.py` parses once and caches the answer against the package files' own sizes and modification times. Warm, listing the languages is one `stat` per package and `read_language` parses the single one-entry table that actually holds it; cold, the sweep runs on a worker and the tab opens immediately. Measured on the installed game: **constructor 3.7 s → 0.14 s, Load 3.6 s → 0.69 s** including the 16 MB parse and a reference language. The cache is derived and always safe to delete — a stale one is never used, because the fingerprint has to match first.
- Moving that sweep onto a worker introduced a crash worth recording. A `QThread` parented to the tab is destroyed with the tab, and destroying a *running* one is an access violation — exactly what closing or reloading the tab mid-sweep would do. It surfaced as `Windows fatal exception: access violation` in the test run rather than in the panel. The worker threads are unparented and held in a module-level set until they finish; Qt severs the signal to the dead receiver on its own.
- **Translation Studio ignored the archive root the user configured** and always read `corpus.game_root()` — the environment variable or a hardcoded install path. Anyone whose game is elsewhere got "No string tables found in the archives" with nothing to act on. It reads the live value off the Settings widget now, the way Placement Studio does, and says where to set it when there is none.
- **Layered materials render as layers instead of collapsing to one tone.** The layer parameter says which channel of the mask selects it, and that now outranks anything read off the mask binding. `_detailMaskTexture` resolves to a fixed `b`, and letting that overwrite the layer put `_detailDiffuseMaskR`, `G` and `B` all on blue — a fully layered helmet came out flat. A mask's own channel is still the fallback for layers that name none. The .NET viewport composites them through a new `NetMaterialLayerCompiler`, with an orientation proof beside it so a flipped layer cannot pass unnoticed.
- **223 of 3,148 sampled parts rendered an `_o` overlay as their albedo** while their own `_baseColorTexture` sat bound alongside. Base-colour selection asked which slot a texture arrived in, and the slot list was a proxy for the real question — whether the part supplies a primary base colour of its own family — which never covered gloves, hoods, boots, bags or rings. It asks the real question now.
- **Faces rendered at 0.45 of their own albedo's brightness.** A field that covers 42% of the sheet, and that appears in 13 bindings across 4,665 sampled assets, was being read as metalness saying a face is 100% metal. 24 of 72 sampled heads classified metal rather than skin, and a metal classification takes the lower ambient floor and the cut diffuse. It is not coverage and is no longer read as one.
- **Brass and gold resolved to grey steel.** A chromatic metal tint was damped to 5%, and 217 of 280 sampled metal parts carry one. The tint reaches the surface through a luma-normalised hue shift now, so it keeps the material's own brightness; the painty colourise path stays suppressed for metals, which is what the damping was protecting against.

- **The studio's name lost its ampersand.** Tab bars and menu items read `&` as a mnemonic marker: it vanishes and underlines the next letter, so "Placement & Animation Studio" was drawn as "Placement_Animation Studio" — which reads as the tool's actual name rather than as a rendering artifact. Titles are still stored unescaped, because window titles and labels would show a doubled `&` literally; the escaping is applied by `as_label()` at the two places that draw them.
- **Opening the studio froze the app for about nine seconds.** Three archive reads ran with nothing painting and nothing answering the mouse. The clip index (4.8 s) walked all 33 package tables on a `QThread`, and since the walk is pure Python the GIL made that starve the UI exactly as badly as calling it inline — measured at 19 event-loop ticks where 150 were due. Its completion slot then read this character's extra weapons (5.9 s) and action charts (2.3 s) straight through on the UI thread. All three now advance a slice per event-loop turn, the way `rig_files.scan_rig_files` already did for the rig walk, and the clip browser shows a progress bar over the packages. Same total time, and the window paints and answers throughout.
- Residual, and not from this layer: `parse_archive_pamt` parses one package table per call, so the largest (0009, 419,660 entries) is a single 1.1 s step that no amount of slicing above it can break up. Worst-case UI latency on open measured 1.3 s, down from the whole nine seconds.
- **Disabled buttons in the studio were unreadable rather than greyed out.** "Bind pose" until a clip is loaded, "Play selected" until a row is picked: the app theme draws a disabled button at #6f7680 on #252526, a 3.3:1 contrast ratio, and against this window's darker chrome it was reported as simply not visible. The studio's own stylesheet now sets 5.1:1 for them, still plainly dimmer than an enabled button so the state reads at a glance.
- **Two tests failed only on CI, for reasons that had nothing to do with what they check.** `test_qa_timeout_and_cleanup_are_enforced` built its QA-owned root under `tmp_path`, and CI runs pytest with `--basetemp=$RUNNER_TEMP/...`, which on a GitHub runner is `D:\a\_temp` and not under `%TEMP%`. `Remove-QAOwnedPath` refuses twice and the *first* refusal is that the owned root is outside the system temp directory, so the test caught the wrong refusal and exited 11. Its roots now come from the real system temp, which is where the guard's precondition lives. Verified by running it with a `--basetemp` on another drive, which reproduces the CI failure locally.
- `test_deferred_close_quits_application_after_hidden_window_is_finalized` gave the deferred close 600 ms before exiting 17. That close polls every 100 ms, so the budget was about six event-loop ticks, and a shared runner missed enough of them to report "the app did not quit" when the app was only slow. The timer is a hang guard rather than a performance assertion, so it is 30 s now, and the same tightness in `test_startup_archive_path_async` — 250 ms — went with it.

- **The last red test on CI was a stale expectation, not a regression.** `test_prefab_corpus_report_probes_same_length_placement_edits` still asserted that the experimental length-changing placement rebuild *succeeds*. It has been refused since `a7d92987`, which replaced in-place splicing with exact pointer relocation and therefore requires a prefab that decodes all the way through. `_prefab_profile_payload` is a hand-assembled byte string with no real type table, so the walk stops on an implausible type count and the rebuild declines — and `a7d92987`'s own message says that outcome is the point, having updated the equivalent expectation in `test_archive_relationships.py` and missed this one.
- Bisected rather than assumed: it passes at `d19d0815` and fails from `a7d92987` on. The test now asserts the refusal and its message, and pins that same-length editing is unaffected, which is the capability that actually ships — it moves no bytes, so no pointer can go stale.

- **A tool-tab window reached past its own domain module into `cdmw.core`.** `tools/placement_studio/window_rig_behaviour.py` imported `PoseModifierError` from `cdmw.core.posemodifier_xml`, which the Layer Rules forbid for a UI surface. It now takes the name from `rig_behaviour.py` beside it — the same shape every sibling already used, `window_constraints.py` through `constraints.py`. Same class object either way, so nothing changes at runtime.
- `test_tool_tab_ui_modules_do_not_import_core_implementations` keeps it fixed. It keys on whether a module imports PySide6 rather than on a file-name pattern, so a new window is covered the day it is written, and it covers the three `tools/` packages that register shell tool tabs — the general import-boundary guards all scope their `rglob` to `cdmw/`, which is why this went unseen. Verified against the unfixed file: it fails there and passes here.
- Corrected the note added to `architecture.md` one commit ago. It said these tabs were "the one place a UI surface reads `cdmw.core` directly" and called the boundary unresolved. That overstated it: `format_explorer` never imported `cdmw.core` at all, `translation_studio` confined it to `catalogue.py`, and `placement_studio` confined it to its domain modules — one file was the exception, not the rule. The section now describes the structure that is actually there.

- **A footer can sit before the blob's trailer run, and the close rule allowed only one or the other.** 39 more prefabs stopped at a median 95% of the way through on leftovers like `5c 00 00 00 01 de 00 00 00` — four bytes of footer, then a five-byte trailer record. Every one of them had *already recovered every object*; the object counts before and after the fix are identical, and only the verdict changes. That verdict is what switches editing on, so files that were read correctly were being refused on their last nine bytes.
- Writing the guard caught the rule being looser than its own docstring claimed. Exact consumption — every remaining byte belonging to a record — is not sufficient once six-byte records are allowed, because the width absorbs one arbitrary byte per record and the footer skip supplies the rest: `5c 00 00 00 01 de 00 00 00 99 99` reads as footer, record and spare byte. The six-byte record is now matched **exactly** against the single form the corpus contains, unlike the five-byte one whose u32 payload genuinely varies. A second six-byte record appearing later surfaces as a partial walk, which is the safe direction to be wrong in. Tightening it cost none of the completions.
- Both tail rules moved to `cdmw/core/prefab_blob_tail.py`. `prefab_binary.py` was 12 lines under its ratchet and this needed 22, and "how the blob closes" is a real seam rather than a place to put the overflow.
- **Completion 878 → 922 of 1,500 (58.5% → 61.5%)** across the two tail fixes, and the resize round-trip sweep goes 687 over 371 files → **757 over 415, still exact on all of them**.

- **The blob trailer comes in two record widths, and only one was read.** 28 prefabs in a 1,500-file sample stopped exactly seven bytes short of finishing, and every one of them held the same unconsumed tail: `01 01 06 00 00 00 01` — one six-byte trailer record and the terminator. `_is_trailer_run` stepped five bytes per record, so it saw a structure it could not follow where there was nothing left but the close.
- Width following the component family is the same rule the footer search already lives by, so the fix is a second stride rather than a special case. It is still a *run* consuming the whole remainder with at most one byte spare, which is what stops it closing a walk that is merely lost: checked, the widened rule accepted exactly those 28 files and exactly seven bytes in each, and nothing regressed.
- **Completion goes 878 to 906 of 1,500 (58.5% → 60.4%)**, and it cascades into editing: the collection resize sweep rises from 687 round trips over 371 files to **743 over 399, still 743 of 743 byte-exact**. Only single six-byte records are attested in the corpus; the run is a generalisation, made because the five-byte rule needed exactly the same one.

- **The Prefab Inspector's hint line was setting the dialog's minimum width.** An unwrapped `QLabel` demands its whole line, and adding the row-menu sentence pushed that past 1,000px — so the window silently refused to shrink below roughly 1,050px, which is most of a laptop screen. Found by launching the dialog on a real shipped prefab and trying to resize it, not by reading the code. It wraps now, with an explicit two-line minimum height because a wrapped label reports a one-line size hint.
- **Duplicating an object left the cursor at the top of the tree**, so the copy — which reads identically to its original, by design — was unfindable. The selection now lands on the copy and scrolls it into view, which is also the row about to be retargeted.

- **Wide collection headers were being read as narrow ones, silently.** The header's extra byte decides whether the count sits at +1 or +2; reading a wide header as narrow yields the true count shifted up a byte, which is still small enough to look plausible. Worse, the walk then *finishes anyway* — it stops on the trailer when the elements run out — so the file looked fully read while a collection declared 256 times more children than it had. 686 collections in a 1,500-file sample were over-declaring this way.
- The tell is that the misread count is a multiple of 256. Over 1,949 collections in completed walks **no correct narrow count was ever a multiple of 256**, and where wide was correct the extra byte was zero in 85 of 87 cases. But the signal is necessary, not sufficient: one file carries it at a header where *neither* reading matches the elements that follow, and forcing the wide form there costs 5,234 bytes of walk.
- So the decoder does not decide per header. It reads the file, and only where a collection over-declares *and* carries the signal does it read again with those headers taken wide — keeping the retry only if it both completes and leaves fewer collections over-declared. Judging on the whole file rather than the header is what leaves that one file alone: it keeps its narrow reading because the retry is worse, not because the rule was weakened. **66 fewer over-declared collections, no completion lost**, and 91 more collection elements became resizable as a result.

- **The full test suite no longer kills the interpreter partway through, which is why CI had not completed a run since 2026-06-06.** It died at roughly 24% with exit code 3, no traceback, no pytest summary and nothing in the Windows event log, so it read as unattributable. It was never silent: Qt on Windows routes `qFatal` to `OutputDebugString` when it does not believe a console is attached, and under a spawned shell it never does. With `QT_FORCE_STDERR_LOGGING=1` the same run says `QWaitCondition: Destroyed while threads are still waiting`.
- The cause is a leaked Qt object, not a threading bug in the app. `tests/test_dotnet_preview_shared_host.py` builds `DotNetPreviewSessionController` **without a parent** six times — production always gives it one and it dies with the window — so the C++ object outlived its test and was destroyed whenever Python's reference count happened to drop. In a full run that lands partway through the *next* file's Qt construction, inside another Qt operation, and aborts the process. Reproducible with exactly two tests, from `test_renderer_ready_keeps_process_for_nonfatal_material_audit_gaps` into `test_mesh_builder_construction_invariants.py`; either alone passes, which is what made it look like an unrelated crash in whichever file happened to be next.
- An autouse fixture now destroys those controllers at the end of the test that created them. Deterministic ownership rather than a suppression: nothing is skipped, deselected, or reordered.

- **Nine 32-bit multiplications in vendored xatlas now happen in 64 bits.** `ArrayBase` and `BitImage` keep `size`, `capacity`, and `elementSize` as `uint32_t`, so `size * elementSize` was evaluated in 32 bits and only then widened to the `size_t` that `memcpy`, `memmove`, `memset`, and `XA_REALLOC_SIZE` take. One operand in each product is cast first now. Reaching the overflow needs more than 4 GB of element data in a single array, which no asset this tool opens approaches, and the allocation and the copy used the same truncated arithmetic so they would at least have agreed — the reason to fix it is that nine standing high-severity alerts train people to stop reading the security tab.
- Recorded in `native/cdmw_mesh_core/third_party/xatlas/README.md` with the line numbers, because they are the only deviation from upstream and an update would silently drop them. `cdmw-mesh-core` rebuilds clean and the UV bake, import contract, and external-model audit suites pass against the rebuilt binary.

- **`posemodifierdata.xml` scanning missed one of the two comment terminators.** A tolerant parser ends a comment at `--!>` as well as `-->`, and the token pattern only knew `-->`, so a comment spelled that way would not end where the engine ends it: the scan would run straight past it and swallow the markup after it as comment text. The document would lose settings silently rather than fail, which is the worst shape for a module whose contract is that an unedited document re-emits its source byte for byte. Nothing in the shipped file uses that spelling — this is the class of bug that stays invisible until the day it is not. Found by CodeQL as `py/bad-tag-filter`.
- The comment body now comes from its own capture group rather than a fixed `[4:-3]` slice, which would have left a stray `-` behind on the four-character terminator.

- Flagged and locked the ~1% of prefabs whose parse the file does not determine. Most collection headers are locally ambiguous, which is harmless — a wrong width desyncs everything after it and the blob fails to close, so completion picks the right reading. But about one file in a hundred **closes under both readings**, and there a clean-looking walk may simply be the wrong parse, with offsets belonging to the other one. `walk_is_determined` detects those by re-walking with the alternate width, and the inspector now refuses editing on them and says why in the banner rather than offering edits that could land on a plausible wrong field.
- One trap worth recording, because the first version had it: a prefab with no ambiguous collection at all decodes identically both ways, and treating that as "two valid readings" flags nearly every file. It is one reading run twice. The check now requires that a width was actually flipped, and a test pins it — measured at 3 undetermined in 373 complete walks, matching the corpus rate.

- **Fifty-four tests could never have passed on CI, and nobody knew, because CI has not completed a run since 2026-06-06.** All of them assert the output of a native helper that a bare checkout does not contain: `cd-texture-dx` for the material-authority channel artifacts, `cdmw_mesh_core` for the external-model audit's UV bake and MikkTSpace tangents and for the topology delete channel, and `cd_hkx` for the tagfile decode assertions and the editor-dialog fingerprint golden. None of those has a Python fallback, by design, so the tests were not subtly wrong on CI: they were asserting against an empty or differently-sourced result.
- They now skip with a named reason when the helper is absent, which is the convention six other test modules already follow (`pytest.skip("cdmw_mesh_core is not built")`). A skip says what was not covered; a failure that has been red for seven weeks says nothing at all. The helpers are still built and the tests still run in full locally and in the packaging job.
- The `.hkx` editor fingerprint deserves naming separately, because its failure looked like a regression rather than a missing tool. The golden was recorded with `cd_hkx` built. Without it the dialog reports `Source: python_converter_report` instead of `native_rust_cd_hkx`, decodes zero semantic objects instead of one, and drops the entire semantic-writer-gate section, so the SHA-256 over the widget tree was comparing two different dialogs.
- README wording drifted from `test_documentation_and_readme_cover_current_mesh_and_dds_workflows` during the rewrite: the guard pins the exact phrases the in-app About documentation also uses, and the rewrite had paraphrased two of them.

### Docs
- **The Mesh Editor README listed two cleanup tools the rail is not allowed to offer.** It described "display triangulate/quadrangulate helpers" alongside remove doubles, delete loose and the rest, but the service refuses both unless the caller passes `allow_legacy_display_cleanup=True`, which no UI path does. They are legacy and archive-path helpers, and a reader looking for them among the cleanup tools would not find them. The section now says so, rather than implying a tool that would raise if a button were wired to it.

- **The release builder had no written rules, so each session rediscovered them.** `AGENTS.md` gains a "The release builder" section: the build is the only proof the build works, `-NativeHelpersOnly` runs the same provenance and GPU gates in about two minutes for helper and native changes, the helper's protocol contract is never restated in the build script, and a gate that rejects something has to say what disagreed. A `CLAUDE.md` now points at `AGENTS.md` rather than repeating it — Claude reads `CLAUDE.md` and Codex reads `AGENTS.md`, and two copies of a rule set drift the same way two copies of a capability list do.
- **`architecture.md` did not know three of the app's tabs exist.** Its Package Map enumerates `tools/` subpackages, but Placement & Animation Studio, Format Explorer and Translation Studio were absent from it — and all three are registered as lazy shell tool tabs in `cdmw/ui/shell/tool_tabs.py`. A reader would conclude `tools/` holds only backend and harness code. Added them, plus `tools/paa_motion/` and `tools/dotnet_bazel_launcher/`.
- Recorded the boundary that finding exposed rather than papering over it: every test in `tests/test_architecture_import_boundaries.py` scopes its `rglob` to `cdmw/`, so the `tools/` packages sat outside the Layer Rules' enforcement. What that left unguarded is covered in `Fixed` above. A new feature tab still belongs in `cdmw/ui/<feature>/` behind a service.

- **The docs still described a renderer that was deleted.** The resident Vortice migration removed the Python D3D11 preview host, `cdmw/ui/mesh_editor/native_preview_runtime.py`, and the `cdmw_d3d11_preview` native helper, but three docs still routed the reader through them — including `mesh_editor_net_authoritative_renderer_audit.md`, whose whole point is naming which renderer holds authority. It now says there is no second renderer. `cdmw/ui/mesh_editor/README.md` also claimed `start_standalone_native_preview()` rejects launch requests; it is the live entry point into the .NET editor, called from six places.
- Corrected the sparse-update description in `mesh-editing-pipeline.md`: delivery is paced by `cdmw/ui/mesh_editor/dotnet_update_queue.py`, not "one sender thread per Python D3D11 host", and revision support is negotiated as a named capability rather than assumed until an older host is identified.
- **Eight docs existed that the index never mentioned**, so nothing pointed at them — `format-decode-progress`, `format-explorer`, `localization-and-constraint-formats`, `mesh-editor-visual-material-parity-audit`, `paa-motion-format`, `pose-modifier-data`, `translation-studio`, and `bazel-migration`. Added them to `docs/README.md` and put the feature list in alphabetical order so the next insertion has an obvious home.
- Two guards now keep this from recurring. `_documentation_files()` only walked `cdmw/`, which is how a broken `docs/` link sat in `native/cdmw_mesh_core/README.md`; it covers `native/`, `tools/`, `tests/`, and the root docs too, 69 files rather than 50. A new guard fails when a doc is absent from the index.

- **Retired the obvious fix for the largest `.prefab` walk failure**, because measuring it showed it is not one. "Mask exceeds every candidate component" is the single biggest cause, and the arithmetic invites a fix: the failing masks need 9 or more bits, no declared component has that many members, and the root `SceneObject` has 13 — and since `_childSceneObjects` holds child scene objects, resolving those groups to the root type looks obviously right.
- It is not. The cause disappears completely — 158 files to zero — and **completion moves 878 to 880 out of 1,500**, objects recovered up 78, median partial progress up 0.7 points. The wall moves rather than falls: 83 of those files then fail on a bogus string length instead, a step later and no further through the file. The group header resolves and the body immediately does not, which says those groups are not root-shaped and the correct type is probably not in the file's type table at all. Recorded in the dead-ends section so the largest-looking cause does not cost someone else a session: its size is not its cost.

- Validated the relocation machinery that array resizing would have to reuse, by the one method available to it: **shrink a path, grow it back, demand the original bytes**. Over 700 sampled prefabs, **386 of 386 round trips are byte-exact and none differ.** The remaining 314 were skipped rather than failed — mostly partial walks, plus a handful the writer refused outright because a pointee had two possible length fields, which is the writer declining an ambiguous case rather than getting it wrong.
- That is a real result for the resize question even though no resize writer exists yet. Moving bytes, relocating pointers by the self-relative rule, rewriting pointee length fields and correcting the data header all survive a shrink-and-regrow with no drift. Had it not, array resizing would have been off the table outright; instead the foundation is sound and what array resize still lacks is only its own count-writing step and a way to validate *that* — the shipped corpus offers no same-asset one-child-apart pairs to check it against.

- Hunted for an oracle to validate `.prefab` array resizing before writing any of it, and **there isn't one**. Over 3,000 sampled prefabs, only 6 pairs share a type signature and differ by exactly one object — and those are unrelated assets that coincide, not the same asset with a child added (`cd_gimmick_steam_engine_train_rail_0001` against `cd_tower_wooden_set13_06`). There is nothing equivalent to the 10,066 same-asset path pairs that validated the path rewriter against the game's own output.
- That is why array editing stays off, and it is a stronger reason than "not implemented yet": a resize writer moves bytes, relocates pointers, rewrites counts and header sizes, and it would rest entirely on this decoder with no external check. If it is attempted it needs a different kind of validation — remove-then-re-add returning the original bytes, and structural invariants over the whole corpus — rather than ground truth. Worth knowing before the writer is built, not after.

- **Four vendored native libraries were shipping without a notice.** `meshoptimizer`, `MikkTSpace`, `ufbx`, and `xatlas` are all statically linked into `cdmw-mesh-core` and none of them appeared in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md`, which is the file the app actually bundles. They are listed now, with purpose, upstream, and licence.
- ufbx is the one that mattered. The other three carry their upstream notice inside the vendored source, but ufbx's licence does not travel with `ufbx.h`, so the vendored copy had no notice anywhere — and MIT, which is the option this project takes, requires it to be included in copies. The upstream `LICENSE` is vendored beside the source for source distribution, and the notice is reproduced verbatim in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` because that is what ships in the binary.

- Tested my own claim that local collection ambiguity makes a completed `.prefab` walk untrustworthy, and **it does not hold** — the result rehabilitates the decoder rather than undermining it. Over 1,500 prefabs the shipped narrow-first reader completes 916 files, and **913 of those contain at least one locally ambiguous collection site**. Forcing the wide reading at exactly those sites leaves only **11** completing. A wrong width desynchronises everything after it and the blob then fails to close, so completion is **globally determined even where individual sites are not**.
- That matters for what already ships: a complete walk is strong evidence of a correct parse, so the existing path-rewrite and placement-edit capability is better founded than I described it an hour ago. The 11 files that complete under both readings are genuinely undetermined and are now the only known population where a complete walk might still be wrong.

- Tried exhaustive backtracking over candidate components with **"the blob closes exactly"** as the oracle — the one approach left that uses the file's own end rather than an external source. It does not work: over 600 prefabs it closed **5 of 228 failures (+0.8%)**, and 4 of those 5 needed only a single choice point. So the failures are not mis-picked candidates among plausible ones; either no assignment closes the blob, or the walk dies before it ever reaches a choice point.
- That is the seventh `.prefab` hypothesis retired by measurement this session. The walk stays at an honestly measured ~60%, and the value continues to sit where it already did: offset-keyed fixed-size editing on the 95.3% of objects whose component type the file states, with a source digest, expected-old bytes, and no edits offered on inferred types.

## [0.11.0-alpha.2] - 2026-07-28

### Docs
- **The repository is now `CDMW-Full`**, next to the read-only `CDMW-Lite`, and `APP_REPOSITORY_URL` follows it. README was rewritten around what the tool has actually become rather than around what it was when the file was last touched: a status header, a workspace table, an **Architecture** section with process-topology, layering, preview-session and build-path diagrams, and a **File format decoding status** section.
- That status section carries the real numbers — 77 engine formats in the shipped build at 41.7% read and 26.0% write, 65.9%/53.8% weighted by file count — rather than a prose claim that drifts from the manifest. It is written from `schemas/archive_content_capabilities.v1.json`, the same source the Archive Browser reports from, and names what is finished, what is partly decoded and why, and which closed formats would pay off first.
- Version is `0.11.0-alpha.2`. No release is published for it yet; the newest build on the Releases page is still `0.10.0-alpha.2`, and the README says so rather than implying otherwise.
- **The README diagrams were unreadable as rendered.** GitHub's mermaid renderer clips a node label instead of wrapping it, so every label written as one long line lost its tail, and two `Note over` blocks in the preview sequence overlapped the lifelines they spanned. Labels are now short lines broken explicitly with `<br/>`, no label carries a sentence, and the two notes moved into prose underneath, where they were always going to read better than as boxes drawn across a diagram.
- Em dashes are gone from the README. Commas, colons, and parentheses carry the same joins.

### Fixed
- **The packaging job installed .NET 8 while every helper project targets `net10.0-windows`.** A release build on CI could not have published the Mesh Editor or the archive worker. It installs .NET 10 now.

### Docs
- Tried to read `hkReflect` type resolution out of the executable and ruled it out, for a reason that reframes `.prefab` entirely: **most of the component types in shipped prefabs do not exist in the runtime.** Of 488 distinct component type names sampled from 600 archive prefabs, only **75 (15%)** appear as strings in `CrimsonDesert.exe` — and the absent ones include `HDAComponent`, `HoudiniOutput`, `HoudiniParameterBool`, `HoudiniParameterInt`, `HoudiniParameterResourcePath`, `HoudiniSubnetInputSpline`. Those are **Houdini digital-asset authoring types the game never instantiates.** The 75 that are present are scattered rather than tabled: only 8 of 74 consecutive names sit within 80 bytes, so there is no contiguous registry to read.
- So `.prefab` carries authoring-time reflection data, much of it with no runtime counterpart — the same pattern as `.papr` turning out to be a 3ds Max rig export left in the archives. Marker-1 component identity cannot be recovered from a runtime registry that does not contain the types. That closes the last route currently on the table, and it does so with a measurement rather than an opinion.

### Fixed
- **`.papr` now decodes and rebuilds all twenty shipped rigs**, up from nineteen. `cd_m0001_00_circusmachine_boss` found 236 entry starts against a declared 237, and the missing entry is *unparented*: its parent name is zero-length, which the reader was rejecting as a malformed name. Such an entry also carries its transform frame with the flag byte left at zero, so the frame's presence follows from the empty parent rather than from the flag, and the writer now reproduces that rather than deriving it — deriving the flag from `transform is not None` breaks the round trip for exactly that entry.
- A zero-length parent on its own is far too weak a signal to accept: a name followed by two zero bytes occurs inside configuration blocks constantly, and accepting those split real blocks apart in ten of the twenty rigs. The discriminator is the frame itself. `_is_frame` requires the rotation to be a unit quaternion and the scale to be positive and finite, which float noise essentially never satisfies. With that in place all twenty rigs agree with both of the file's own totals — `entry_count` and `record_count` — and all twenty rebuild byte for byte across 2,737 configuration blocks.

### Fixed
- **The first `.pac` preview of a cold archive no longer waits on the name index.** The worker answers the dependency lookup without blocking on that build and reports `secondary_index_pending`; the candidate set it returns is already complete, only the display names are missing. The UI now re-resolves once afterwards on a bounded backoff (2.5 s, three attempts, cancelled if the selection moves), dropping the cached dependency set first so the retry does not read back the incomplete one. Previously the choice was a multi-second stall or permanently unnamed candidates.
- **A queued preview no longer queues behind its own warm-up.** The preview-core service runs one job at a time, so an in-flight prewarm put its remaining seconds *in front* of the request it was meant to accelerate — worse than never warming at all. Flushing a scheduled preview now cancels the prewarm first, and both close paths cancel it too, since the shutdown sequence also runs against windows composed without the archive browser's feature providers.

### Fixed
- **A tool-rail click could take the whole Mesh Editor down.** Revealing a rail page for the first time makes WinForms create its handle and then re-parent every already-realised child onto it; embedded under the host window that `SetParent` has been seen to fail with `ERROR_INVALID_STATE` (5023), and an exception escaping there reaches the UI guard, which exits the process. The reveal now reports the window state the failure actually needs — which children were already realised, who owns them now, and whether that owner is still a window — and leaves the rail usable instead of crashing.
- **Startup realization is explicit rather than a side effect of `OnShown`.** An embedded window runs its startup while still hidden and ends by revealing itself; standalone it still runs from `OnShown`. This removes the case where a placement boot reached the rail after embedding, so the `OnShown` pass could not have covered the pages populated since.
- **A package swap no longer drops Edit Mesh.** Every package builder writes `placement` into `dotnet_scene.json`, so adopting the incoming interaction and comparison modes collapsed the flanks and brought the role-pane selector back whenever a rebuild landed mid-edit, without telling the host. Those modes are live host state, not package content, and are now carried forward and re-asserted after the swap — the same host-owned/package-owned split `ReplaceResidentPackage` already applied to the grid and gizmo toggles.

### Changed
- **Archive tree columns fit their content once, when rows first arrive.** The previous pass sized columns from header text and a coarse row-count heuristic, so `Name` was capped at 360 px and long asset names were ellipsized in the common case. Widths are now measured from the rendered text of the loaded rows — up to 400, indentation included, recursing only where the flat remote view is not active — and the cap on `Name` is 520 px. A user-customized layout still wins and suppresses the fit entirely.

### Docs
- Checked whether the Havok reflection behind `.prefab` is documented, and **it is not**. The version is `Havok_2024` — 107 occurrences in the executable — and the classic reflection registry names are entirely absent: `hkTypeInfoRegistry`, `hkBuiltinTypeRegistry` and `hkDynamicClassNameRegistry` all occur **zero** times, against 191 for `hkReflect` and 54 for `hkSerialize`. This is the newer `hkReflect` system, not the `hkClass` one that public Havok documentation and the community `.hkx` tooling both target.
- So the hopeful reading from the previous commit is closed off: component resolution has to be reverse engineered from this binary like anything else, and existing Havok knowledge is aimed at the wrong system. That is worth knowing before anyone spends a day looking for a spec. The registry itself still has not been located.

### Docs
- Went looking for `.prefab` component-resolution logic in `CrimsonDesert.exe` and found something that may reframe the format: **the reflection system behind it appears to be Havok's, not Pearl Abyss's.** The binary carries `hkReflect` RTTI throughout — `MutableTypeReg`, `BuiltinTypeReg`, `AttributeArrayWithOwnership`, `Version::PatchRegistry`, `Version::PatchSet`, `ClonerCallback` — with a Pearl Abyss layer above it (`AutoReflectBase@pa`, `ReflectObjectIterator@pa`, `IReflectObjectResourceBase@pa`, `CustomAttributeReflectObject@pa`). `ReflectObject` appears 737 times, `SceneObject` 504.
- If component identity is resolved through a Havok **type registry** rather than through declaration order, marker-1 identity may be recoverable from that registry — and `hkReflect` is documented middleware rather than a format that must be derived from bytes. The project already treats Havok formats as `third_party` origin elsewhere. **Not confirmed:** the registry has not been located, and no link between it and the on-disk masks has been established. Recorded as a lead, with the same discipline that has now rejected four `.prefab` hypotheses that looked stronger than this one at the same stage.

### Docs
- Built the `.prefabdata_xml` ↔ `.prefab` pairing and measured it, and **the oracle does not help**. 2,609 stems pair, but **2,607 of them (99.9%) already walk to completion** — only 2 fail. The XML-paired set turns out to be almost exactly the easy subset: small character prefabs with simple roots like `HeadPrefabData` and `NudePrefabData`. The 40% of the corpus that fails has essentially no XML counterpart, so it offers no leverage at all on marker-1 component identity.
- Measured before being built on, which was the point of recording it as unexploited rather than as a plan. Component selection still has no external discriminator, and that remains the single thing standing between the walk and the other 40%.

### Docs
- Found what is very likely the external oracle `.prefab` component selection needs. **`.prefabdata_xml` — 2,613 files — is an XML serialisation of the same reflection data, and 399 of 400 sampled pair with a `.prefab` binary of the same stem.** Each names its root type and members outright: `<HeadPrefabData>` with `SkeletonVariationName`, `FacialAnimationIntensityMask`, `FacialAnimationMask`, `MorphTargetSet`, `SkeletonMorphMask`, `EmotionAnimationSet`. That is the same XML-pairing trick that confirmed `.paprojdesc`, and it is exactly the "external source for marker-1 component identity" the previous commit said was missing.
- Recorded, not yet exploited. The next step is to check across the paired corpus whether the XML names the component the binary omits at marker-1 sites, and if so whether identity is recoverable from member-name signatures rather than from declaration order — the guess that all 247 sampled failures currently rest on.

### Docs
- Attacked `.prefab` component selection and rejected a hypothesis that fit the arithmetic exactly. At **every one of 247 sampled failures the file states no type index** — 100% out-of-range — so the walk is guessing the component by declaration order, and the mask error is the symptom rather than the cause. The masks do look explicable: strip the top set bit and all of them fit the members available (`0x0110` needs 5 against 5, `0x091a` needs 9 against 11, `0x0100` needs 0 against 8), which is a clean enough signature to be convincing.
- It was implemented and measured anyway, and **rejected**: completion moved 59.6% → 59.8%, eight files out of 4,000. The old mask errors were simply replaced by new ones and by blob-string-length desyncs further along, meaning the walk gets past that point and then goes wrong elsewhere. A rule that fits every observed number without improving decoding is not the rule. Recorded so the next attempt does not re-derive it; what is actually needed is an external source for marker-1 component identity, because the file does not carry it at these sites.

### Docs
- Ran collection-header telemetry over 7,121 sites in 2,500 random prefabs, and it **kills both standing hypotheses**. Width is not determined by the element type in the type table — circular, since kind 7 declares the generic `ReflectObjectPtr` — and it is not determined by member metadata either: every collection member carries `flags=7`, and 4 of the 12 distinct `(member name, flags)` tuples require both the narrow and the wide form.
- The finding that matters is neither of those. **6,183 of 7,121 sites are ambiguous** — both widths yield a plausible count — so the shipped plausibility heuristic is choosing arbitrarily at 87% of sites, and is unsound even where a walk completes. Walk-completion percentage was never measuring what it appeared to. If a discriminator exists it is upstream in component selection, where mask-exceeds-candidate is now the largest failure class. Recorded rather than guessed at, and value editing is scoped so it does not depend on the answer.

### Changed
- **The editing bar is three rows of grouped controls instead of a nine-column grid.** A grid ties every row to the same column widths, so `Undo my changes to this point` sitting in column 4 made column 4 that wide on *every* row — which is why `Step:` sat a hand's width from the nudge buttons above it and most of the bar was gap. Each group is its own box now, so a wide button widens only its own group. Writing the mod out moved to the top row, which had the room the bottom one did not, and is not an editing control anyway. Button labels are short, with the sentence on the tooltip: spelled out, five of them wanted 1,052 px and the bar asked for 2,262 in a 1,500 px window, so Qt clipped them and they were unreadable regardless. The bar now asks for 1,082 against 2,262. Three rows, not two: the controls need about 3,050 px between them, so two rows means 1,525 each and it clipped at any ordinary window width. Each row answers one question — what am I editing and how far does a step go, move it, act on it. The angle boxes size themselves from the longest value they can hold rather than a hard-coded width, which is what left `pitch 0,0` short of its last digit.

### Fixed
- **Changing character stalled for several seconds.** Two causes, both things that were cheap against the old stand-in body and are not against a real one. Clipping was measured *live* whenever no clip was loaded — 5.5 million triangle tests against a 28,316-triangle anatomy, 6.2 s, on every mesh refresh, to answer a question nobody had asked; it is measured on request now, which is what the `Check Fit/Clipping` button and its tooltip already said. And the chart index was rebuilt from all 212 charts on every call and called twice per switch, 1.4 s of it, re-deriving something that had not changed. Switching character goes from 3.9 s to 2.0 s, and the six-second stall is gone entirely.

### Changed
- `Show:` opens on `in hand (held)`. The question this tool answers is where a weapon sits while carried *and* what the hand does with it; the held pose shows both.
- Move-dialog rows no longer count their files. How many takes a row covers is a property of the game's recording rather than anything a modder decides, and it was on almost every row. The lane heading still counts them and the tooltip still lists them by name.

### Changed
- Move-dialog rows are much shorter. `Drawing the weapon · dual swords ← two-handed sword (2 files)` is seventy characters of which four carry the decision; it now reads `Draw · dual swords (2 files)`. The action is a word rather than a sentence, because the row already sits under a heading naming the situation; family names are short; and the family being borrowed *from* is named only when rows actually differ, since a swap runs in one direction and repeating it on every row restates the sentence at the top of the dialog.
- Damian's rapier families had no readable names and showed as `rpr` and `2rpr`.

### Fixed
- `weaponout`/`weaponin` — the underscore-less spelling a run of mounted clips uses — had no entry in the action vocabulary, so those rows fell through to raw file words and read `Att skill weaponout f handr swing forward`. The switch action is also matched before the sheathe now: `weapon_ing` contains `weapon_in`, so order is what keeps a switch from reading as a put-away.

### Docs
- Walked three more shot-data serialisers. `ProjectilePhysicsShotData` reads `+0x20` u32, `+0x24` u32, `+0x28` u32 and four u8 at `+0x2c`–`+0x2f`; `ProjectileGuideShotData` reads five u32 at `+0x20`–`+0x30`. Their vftables sit exactly `0x28` apart at `0x04f22848`, `…70`, `…98`, `…c0`, which confirms these are consecutive **sibling** classes — and so the read/write slot groups seen earlier in one vftable dump were the neighbours' tables, not one class's.
- The owning class is not among them. `ClientProjectile_Shot` (vftable `0x04b64768`) is the likeliest container, but the triple scanner mis-parses its serialiser, returning a single implausible 515-byte read, so it needs following by hand. Recorded rather than guessed at.

### Docs
- Walked `ProjectileShotData`'s serialiser to the end. Its vftable holds a matching **read/write pair at slots 2 and 3** (rva `0x01ea11e0` and `0x01ea1260`), and both serialise the same four fields: `+0x10` u32, `+0x14` u32, `+0x18` u8, `+0x19` u8 — ten bytes of payload in an object whose first `0x10` bytes are the vptr and base data that never reach the file. Slots 7/8 and 12/13 are the same read/write pattern for neighbouring classes with 7 and 5 fields, and slots 4, 9 and 14 point into `.rdata` rather than code, which is what marks where this vftable ends and the next begins.
- The consequence is the useful part: **ten serialised bytes cannot be the top-level record** of `projectileshotinfo.paproj`, whose measured period is 156 bytes. So `ProjectileShotData` is a component inside a larger record, not the record itself, and the earlier reading of that 156-byte period as "one struct per repeat" was wrong. The remaining work is to find the class that owns it and walk that class's serialiser the same way — the method now being proven end to end on a real class.

### Fixed
- **Two naming shapes the convention turned out not to be consistent about, both of which hid real draws.** A run of mounted clips spells the action `weaponout`/`weaponin` rather than `weapon_out`/`weapon_in`, and reading only the usual spelling classified every one of them as ordinary locomotion. And the family is the third token only when the context is folded into the character slot — Kliff's `cd_prh_swd_...` — where Damian keeps her name and adds the context after it, `cd_damian_rd_prh_lswd_...`, which put `rd` in the family slot and made all of her mounted clips unplaceable. Damian's one-handed draws go from 6 to 28, and her swappable draw pairs from 8 to 10; Kliff's go from 54 to 56.

### Fixed
- A Move-dialog row named only the clip it replaces, so `Drawing the weapon · dual swords` sat next to a Watch button that plays a two-handed draw from the back. Reading the row as a description of what you are about to watch is the obvious reading and it was wrong. Rows now name both — `Drawing the weapon · dual swords ← two-handed sword` — using the same arrow the tooltip has always used for target ← donor.

### Fixed
- The clip browser's scan button and the `Only draws for this spot` checkbox were clipped mid-word. Three controls shared a row and want 216, 312 and 338 px at this DPI — 890 with spacing, in a lane that opens at 620. Qt answers a row it cannot fit by clipping rather than wrapping, so the button gets a row of its own.

### Docs
- Disassembled into `.paproj`'s loader and found the mechanism that defines the on-disk layout. Following the complete object locator to `ProjectileShotData`'s vftable (rva `0x04f22848`) and disassembling the code that installs it shows **a field-by-field deserialiser, not a `memcpy`**: `lea rdx, [rdi + offset]; mov r8d, size; call qword ptr [rax + 8]; test al, al; je fail`, repeated once per field, where `rdi` is the object, the stream's vtable slot `+8` is `Read(dest, size)`, and the function returns `bool`. Four fields are legible in the tail reached so far — `+0x10` as 4 bytes, `+0x14` as 4, `+0x18` as 1, `+0x19` as 1.
- That is the method settled rather than the format decoded. Field sizes turn out to be explicit in the code rather than something to infer from value shapes, which is what makes this tractable at all. The complete field list is not extracted: a follow-up scan windowed onto a neighbouring serialiser instead of this one, so the offsets above are a sample and not the struct. `capstone` was installed into the venv for this and is not recorded in any requirements file.

### Fixed
- **Damian was shown Kliff's action charts.** The pinned baseline holds one character's four, so the raw chart text on screen belonged to somebody else, and the socket list built from it filtered them out by model and came up empty — which read as "this character has no animations routed anywhere". His own five are in the packages. Charts are indexed alongside armour now, and the character's own are loaded when they are selected: Damian goes from 0 sockets listed to 4. Only the selected character's are read, because Kliff has 101 and paying for both bodies to show one of them would be a launch-time cost for nothing.

### Docs
- Located `ProjectileShotData`'s RTTI in `CrimsonDesert.exe` — TypeDescriptor at rva `0x05f6a810`, complete object locator at `0x053c168c`, class hierarchy at `0x053c0df0` — and recorded two facts the next attempt needs. The hierarchy declares a single entry, the class itself, so **`ProjectileShotData` has no base class** and its layout is flat, with no inherited prefix to account for; that fits `projectileshotinfo.paproj` being the one file with a clean fixed period. But the class is polymorphic, so the in-memory object begins with an 8-byte vptr that cannot appear in the file — **the on-disk record is a serialised form, not a `memcpy` of the class**, and any field offsets taken from a constructor will be shifted relative to the file.
- No disassembly was done: `capstone`, `dumpbin` and `objdump` are all absent from this checkout, and adding one is a tooling decision rather than something to slip into a format commit. That is recorded in the manifest so the gap is visible rather than looking like unfinished analysis.

### Added
- A Move-dialog row whose animation was borrowed from the other playable character now says `· borrowed`, in amber, and its tooltip explains the trade: the clip plays because the two rigs share most of their bones, but it was authored for different proportions, so reaching and contact may be a little off. Amber rather than red — it is a caveat, not a fault. The file names it already showed stay in the tooltip underneath.

### Docs
- Located the `.paproj` loader, which reframes the format. `CrimsonDesert.exe` carries `paproj` twice, `paprojdesc` once, `ProjectileInfo` 44 times, and **79 MSVC RTTI class names** matching `.?AV*Projectile*@pa@@`. Those names map one-to-one onto the file names — `CommonProjectile` to `commonprojectile.paproj`, `CommonProjectile_AttachToActor` to `projectileinfo_attachtoactor.paproj`, `_Repeat` and `_Wave` likewise, and `ProjectileShotData` to `projectileshotinfo.paproj`.
- That answers why the earlier analysis kept failing: **each file is a table of one projectile subclass, not one shared record type.** No single record size could fit the set, and only `projectileshotinfo` showed a clean period (156 bytes) because it is the only file holding a single simple struct. The manifest now records the mapping, the four ruled-out readings, and that the remaining work is per-file — recovering each struct's field offsets from the constructors the RTTI names point at.

### Added
- A weapon swap can borrow the other playable character's animations where the body has no counterpart of its own.
