The two rigs share 403 bone names of Kliff's 434 and Damian's 448, and a Kliff sword draw resolves against Damian's skeleton with exactly the coverage it has on Kliff's — 89.6% either way — so the clip plays. It is a fallback and never displaces a clip authored for the body being edited: `.paa` keys are bind-pose deltas in bone-local axes, so the same rotations on different proportions land slightly differently, and contact points are where that shows.
- What it is worth is worth stating plainly. It gains Kliff two draws and Damian none — not for want of donors, since 1,002 of Kliff's clips qualify, but because a swap rewrites files the game already asks for and Damian *has* only 8 one-handed draws against Kliff's 60. The ceiling on Damian is targets, not donors, and no amount of borrowing raises it.

### Fixed
- **Damian could not swap animations at all.** The swap deliberately restricts itself to the player's own clips — an unfiltered sweep once rewrote 121 files including every boss's draw — but the list of what those are called was hard-coded to Kliff's two prefixes. Damian's clips are named differently, so every target was skipped and the dialog truthfully reported that no animation had a counterpart. The prefixes now follow the model; an unknown character falls back to its own token alone, because being wrong here means rewriting somebody else's animations.
- **Damian's weapon was drawn with the wrong hand.** The part being edited defaulted to `CD_MainWeapon_Sword_R` outright. Kliff's swords are all right-handed so the two agreed by luck; Damian has a left-handed set, and choosing one left the right-hand row selected — the left-hand sword on screen while the animation drove the right arm. The default now matches the hand *and* the kind the chosen weapon is authored for, and a previous choice gives way when it no longer suits, since both characters have a `CD_MainWeapon_Sword_R` for it to survive as.
- **Bows, shields, muskets and torches drew nothing when selected.** Their mesh path was built by reading one bit — two-hand if the name contained `_02_`, one-hand otherwise — so every other category resolved to a file that does not exist. They still appeared in the list, because their socket file is real. The folder now comes from the number the weapon carries, which takes Kliff from about ten weapons that render to 35, and every category is named rather than showing `(?)`. A weapon whose mesh is nowhere in the archives is left out of the list instead of being offered as a dead entry.
- Fist weapons ship as two meshes, one per hand, where every other category carries the side suffix on the socket file alone. Both spellings are tried.
- Loading a character resolved its bone palette twice per mesh — once for the primary bone and again for the second — and the scan behind it walks the whole file. It is resolved once.
- The retarget row's button was pushed off the right-hand edge of the window: it had been given the same "may shrink" treatment as the combos beside it, and two stretching combos then took every pixel. A control that is merely narrow is recoverable; one that is off screen is not.
- The rig dropdown elided its entries down the middle, so `10_pgw (playable)` read as `10_pgw...yable)` — unreadable at both ends and identical to its neighbours.

### Docs
- Took a serious run at `.paproj` and did not crack it, so the manifest now records what is ruled out rather than implying the work is undone. Stride analysis finds no fixed record in eight of the nine files — their strongest period is 4 bytes, which is the noise floor of a float array rather than a record boundary. The ninth, `projectileshotinfo`, does show a real 156-byte period from around offset 284, confirmed by its 312 harmonic and by `-1.0` markers sitting at consistent offsets inside it, but agreement is 0.548 against a 0.5 baseline and the fields decode as denormal noise, so the period is real while the layout over it is not established.
- The reason this is harder than `.papr` is worth recording: `.papr` has a `record_count` in its header that no decoding rule can influence, and every candidate grammar could be checked against it. `.paproj` has no such counter, so a plausible-looking layout would have nothing to falsify it — which is exactly the situation that produced the discarded readings earlier in this format work.

### Added
- Decoded `.paprojdesc` completely. Despite the name it has nothing to do with projects: these are **projectile** files, and this one is the index under `actionchart/` that says which projectile-info modules each package loads. It is a `u16` string count, then strings in the `.paac` convention (a length byte that counts the trailing NUL), a `u32` package count and module total, one 6-byte row per package, and a flat `u16` module list. All 139 bytes of the shipped file are accounted for and it rebuilds byte for byte.
- It also has something almost no format here has: **ground truth**. `actionchart/xml/description/projectilepackagedescription.xml` is the source it was compiled from, so the corpus gate checks the decode against the XML rather than against itself — two packages, `ProjectileInfoPackage` and `GimmickPackage`, each loading the same three modules. That is what turned a plausible reading of the trailing 32 bytes into a confirmed one.
- `.paproj` stays undecoded, but its manifest row now says something useful instead of "container identified, record layout unknown". These are the nine projectile-definition files the index names, 2.1 MB in total. They carry no strings at all, so the content is numeric records — speeds, gravity, lifetimes and collision sizes on the evidence of the float values. What is ruled out is recorded too: the leading `u32` differs per file and does not track file size, and no header/record-size pair between 8 and 128 bytes divides any file evenly at the count it declares, so the records are not fixed width or the header is longer than it looks.

### Added
- **The Placement Studio draws a whole character.** The body was the coat and trousers the baseline pins, so the figure had no hands, no feet and no face — and the thing a weapon's clipping was measured against was a coat with no arms in it. The bare figure and its head come from the packages instead; clothing appears only as it is worn, which is also what makes a fit check mean anything.
- Surfaces are lit by the direction they face rather than by depth, so a limb reads as round instead of merging into the chest behind it, and each worn piece is tinted apart from the body and from the others.
- Clip rows, weapons, parts, sockets and rigs are named the way a person would say them, with the game's own identifiers one hover away — `cd_boarmimic_basic_00_00_nor_move_walkfast_turn180l_stt_00` reads as `Boarmimic - Move - Walkfast - Turn 180 L (start)`. A trim rather than a translation: what is left still names the file on disk, which the search box matches and the mod is written against.
- The Help panel covers the whole tool. It described one job and never mentioned the other five tabs or any viewport control; it now maps both, and is held to a readable measure instead of filling the window.

### Changed
- **A `.pac` says which bone drives each vertex, and it is read rather than inferred.** The studio matched influence slots to whichever bone sat nearest the average of the vertices using them. That held while the body was a coat; a hand has fifteen bones inside a few centimetres, and the guess paired fingers with the wrong knuckles and tore the mesh apart the moment a pose moved. Every mesh carries a palette of `.pab` name hashes that resolves against the rig exactly — 189 bones in Kliff's body, 206 in Damian's, 77 in a coat — and in each file exactly one of thousands of candidate byte runs resolves completely. The earlier scan that concluded otherwise read only the first 4 KB, which is where bodies keep theirs and armour does not.
- **Two influences per vertex instead of one, so joints bend.** The second bone is at byte 24 of the vertex record: where the second weight is zero it is zero 99.3% of the time, and where set it resolves a median 0.15 m from the primary against 0.65 m for a random bone. Bytes 32 and 33 track the third and fourth weights just as tightly but are *not* bones — read as slots they land 0.44 m away, worse than chance — so those two are left undecoded rather than guessed at. Peak triangle stretch falls on all six measured poses across both characters.
- Detail budgets were calibrated against a 5,379-triangle stand-in for the body. Against a real 38,944-triangle character the moving budget kept 11% — shards with the limbs missing — and the size floor charged 3.25 px a face, which took the head off as soon as the camera pulled back. Both recalibrated; the headroom came from the face pass, which was reading numpy scalars one at a time at 4.2 µs a triangle.
- Clips & animation is two lanes rather than one column of squeezed boxes, group headings are bold and tinted so they read as headings, and `Match animations` is now `Find which draws fit (~30s)` — it only measures, and the old name read as a command that changes something.

### Fixed
- Controls that overlapped each other. A combo asks to be as wide as its longest entry, and Qt answers an impossible row by drawing widgets over each other rather than shrinking them: two socket names and a button did exactly that, and a six-control filter row clipped `Only draws for this spot` to `Only draw`.
- The "What is this for?" badge stylesheet was built from three concatenated strings of which the last was not an f-string, so its `}}` stayed two braces and Qt rejected the whole rule silently.

### Fixed
- Cracked the last `.papr` holdout. `cd_m0001_00_circusmachine_boss` found 236 entry starts against a declared 237 and had been rejected rather than guessed at since the format was first read. The entry it missed is **unparented** — its parent name is zero length — which the entry scanner rejected as a malformed name. Such an entry also carries its transform frame with the flag byte left at zero, so the frame's presence follows from the empty parent rather than from the flag; the writer now reproduces that instead of deriving it, which is what keeps the round trip exact.
- A zero-length parent on its own is far too weak a signal to scan on: a name followed by two zero bytes occurs inside configuration blocks constantly, and accepting those split real blocks apart in ten of the twenty rigs. What makes it safe is checking the frame that follows — the rotation must be a unit quaternion and the scale positive and finite, which float noise essentially never satisfies. The block that first exposed the entry decoded cleanly the moment the split was right, and every other rig was left untouched.
- **All twenty shipped rigs now parse, tile to their declared `entry_count`, rebuild byte for byte, and decode every configuration block** — 2,737 of 2,737, with 1,046 expression controllers recovered, and all twenty reproducing their declared `record_count` exactly. `.papr` has no unread bytes and no rejected files left.

### Added
- **`.papr` is fully decoded.** All 2,541 configuration blocks across the nineteen parsable rigs now consume exactly, from the 682 (26.8%) that matched a single canonical shape when this started, and every chain in every rig reports every byte accounted for. 906 3ds Max expression controllers come out as readable text — the rule each driven bone actually runs.
- The last two constructs: `09 03` opens a scope and sets the channel count exactly as `0a 04` does, and `01 03` is a driver list that omits the sentinel and takes `3 + channels` floats rather than `4 + channels`. They had been refused for two commits because an earlier reading had the right idea and the wrong float run, which raised coverage to 98.0% while dropping header agreement from nine rigs to seven. The correct run turned up in `B_Gluteusmaximus_L_02`, a 31-byte block with no drivers at all, where the three floats sit alone between the count and the next record.
- **All nineteen rigs now reproduce their declared `record_count` exactly**, from bear at 12 records to golem_imp_boss at 4,317. Nothing in the grammar can influence that header total, so nineteen independent agreements is the strongest statement available that these are the file's own rules. The corpus gate asserts both the full block count and the number of agreeing rigs. The manifest's `decode` grade for `.papr` moves from `partial` to `full`.

### Docs
- Rewrote the `.papr` block section of the format guide, which still described the block as opaque and the grammar as unknowable. It now carries the record table, the driver-list and bound-node layouts, three real formulas next to what they mean, and — the part worth keeping — how any of it was checked. Coverage alone proves little, because permissive rules also consume bytes; the check that counts is the header's own `record_count`, which no decoding rule can influence, and nine rigs reproduce theirs exactly. That section also records the bound-node case, where the header settled a question the byte shapes could not, and the `09 03` reading that reaches higher coverage but costs header agreement and is therefore refused.

### Docs
- Brought the `.papr` row of the capability manifest up to date with what the decoder actually does now. Its evidence still said "682 of 2,541 config blocks are one fully decoded 9-record shape" and its remaining column still said "block contents also stay opaque", both of which stopped being true several commits ago. It now records 97.7% of blocks consuming exactly, the 894 recovered expression controllers, and the header-`record_count` agreement across nine rigs that is the reason to believe any of it — plus the 59 blocks that genuinely still refuse. The `decode` grade stays `partial` on purpose: the rubric reserves `full` for "no unexplained spans in the supported path", and 59 blocks is not none.

### Added
- Took `.papr` block decoding to **2,482 of 2,541 blocks — 97.7%, from 96.1%** — and chains with every byte accounted for to 94.9%. Three small corrections, all of the same kind: bytes read as fixed zeros that are not. A bound node's lead byte is a flag that is 0 in most rigs and 1 in a few; its name is occasionally empty, meaning an unbound slot rather than a corrupt record; and a driver list may legitimately declare no drivers at all.
- The evidence for these is much stronger than coverage alone. **Nine rigs now decode end to end and all nine reproduce their declared `record_count` exactly**, up from four, and they include the large ones: golem_imp_boss at 4,317 records, machinetank and warrobot at 1,660 each, golemdragon at 891. Nothing in this grammar can influence that header total, so nine independent agreements at that size is what makes the rules credible rather than merely permissive. The corpus gate asserts the count of agreeing rigs, so a future change that buys coverage by losing agreement fails.
- The last 59 blocks are left refused on purpose. `09 03` (56) reads plausibly as an opener carrying two bytes, after which `01 03` looks like a driver list with no sentinel — and read that way the corpus reaches 98.0%. But the number of rigs agreeing with their own header then falls from nine to seven, so the limit run in that shape is not the `4 + channels` one and the reading is not yet right. A block this module admits it cannot read is a better answer than coverage bought at the cost of the only external check there is.

### Added
- Cracked the last two big `.papr` constructs. **2,441 of 2,541 blocks now consume exactly — 96.1%, from 78.4%** — and chains with every byte accounted for went from 85.6% to 91.1%. `02 01` and `02 02`, 452 blocks between them, turn out to be the bound node already decoded for `01 01` and `04 01`: a zero byte, a name, and `4 + channels` limit floats. They had looked different only because the earlier reading gave `04 04` a limit run it does not have, which shifted every offset after it and made the byte following the record header look like a count.
- So the split is by tag rather than by shape: tags 1 to 5 at type 1 or 2 are bound nodes, while 0x10, 0x11 and 0x12 at the same types are real records — a scalar, an expression and a name. All four rigs that decode end to end still reproduce their declared `record_count` exactly (bear 12, deerila 324, golemhorse 377, horse 353), which is what says the generalisation is the file's rule and not a shape that happens to fit.
- The 100 blocks still short stop at four constructs. `09 03` (56) is a record whose payload matches no shape here. The other three are all places where a byte this grammar expects to be zero is not — a bound node followed by 01 (37), a `01 03` frame followed by a count (3), and one zero-length string — which looks like a single missing idea: several payloads probably begin with a count, and the decoded forms are the count-is-one case. That is not pinned down, so they fail loudly rather than being guessed past.

### Added
- Decoded another 135 `.papr` blocks: **1,992 of 2,541 now consume exactly, 78.4% against 73.1%**, and the share of chains with every byte accounted for went from 75.4% to **85.6%**. Three rules did it. The two driver tags turn out to differ — `03 04` takes the limit floats after its list and `04 04` takes none, and reading limits for both swallowed the records that followed. A driver payload can also carry a bound node: a zero byte, a name, and its own limit run. And `01 03` is a bare frame of one zero byte and three floats.
- The bound node is the interesting one, because the header settled it rather than a guess. Read as a record it looks like any other, but `record_count` — the file's own total, which nothing in the grammar can influence — then overshoots by 6 on deerila and 11 on each horse rig, exactly the number of bound nodes each holds. Not counted, all three land on their declared total to the record. Four rigs now decode end to end and all four agree with their header: bear 12, deerila 324, golemhorse 377, horse 353.
- The 549 blocks still short stop at a handful of constructs, and the module records where to go next: `02 01` (236 blocks) and `02 02` (216) look like the same bound-node shape with a different leading byte, where the byte after the record header is probably a count rather than the fixed zero the decoded forms use.

### Changed
- Replaced the standing explanations at the top of both rig tabs with a badge and a "What is this for?" button. Between them the warning banner, the purpose paragraph, the can/cannot box and the no-preview note were spending roughly 300px of a 780px panel on text that is read once and in the way thereafter — and burying the one fact that matters as a sentence among five. Driven bones now leads with an orange **EXPERIMENTAL** badge and Rig behaviour with a green **TAKES EFFECT** one, so whether an edit reaches the game is visible without reading anything. The tables get the space back.
- The rest moved into a dialog that is written to be scanned rather than read: short headings, bullets, and worked examples in two columns. Driven bones explains what a driven bone actually is (deformation, not hair — 259 of 471 chains against 5 jiggle), shows real formulas next to what they mean (`Local_Euler_Y*1.5-1.7` → "follow 1.5x the driver's Y rotation, offset -1.7"), lists what it can and cannot change, and sets out the evidence behind the badge. Rig behaviour explains what each section governs, the shapes a value takes, and the two things that surprise people: one block often serves several characters, and a section can be switched off for a skeleton. The can/cannot list is generated from the same `CAPABILITIES` tuple the code is built around, so the dialog cannot promise something no function behind it can do.

### Fixed
- Stopped the Studio freezing when Driven bones or Rig behaviour is first opened. Finding the rig files means walking the archive package tables — 1.6 million entries, about four seconds — and that was happening inline on the UI thread, so the window locked up on the click. It now runs as a generator stepped from the Qt event loop, yielding every 20,000 entries, with a progress bar in the status bar and the panel saying what it is waiting for. A worker thread was tried first and does not work: the walk is pure Python and holds the GIL, so the UI heartbeat still showed a 12-second gap; stepping it from the event loop brings that down to the cost of one package's table parse. The click itself now returns in about 2 ms.
- The result is also written to disk, so only the first session ever pays for the walk. It is under a megabyte — twenty `.papr` files and the pose-modifier descriptor — and reloads in about 6 ms, stamped with each package file's size and modification time so a game update invalidates it rather than leaving the panels showing the previous install. A damaged or stale cache is treated as a miss, never as an error.

### Added
- Decoded most of the `.papr` configuration block, which had been the opaque part of the format. It reads as a flat stream of 3-byte `(tag, type, value)` records where the type says whether a payload follows: driver lists (`u8 count`, then `u16` name and `f32` weight per driver, a sentinel, and a run of limit floats), plain name references, and — the useful one — 3ds Max expression controllers. **1,857 of 2,541 blocks now consume exactly, against the 682 that matched the single canonical 27-byte shape recognised before**: 26.8% to 73.1%. For the player rigs it turns 2 of 71 fully-decoded chains into 58 of 71, and 1 of 73 into 63 of 73, so the Driven bones tab no longer reads "partial" down the whole column.
- The expression payload is the actual rule each driven bone runs, and 894 of them now come out as text: `-Local_Euler_Z*3+30.5`, `amin(Local_Euler_Z*5.5+20) 8`, `(Local_Euler_Y-(degToRad 63.748))/2+(degToRad 63.748)`. The Driven bones detail table gained a Formula column beside Follows and Weight, because "Bip01 L Clavicle_Front follows Bip01 L UpArmTwist at 30%" is a fact and "at 1.5x its Y rotation, offset -1.7" is the thing a modder came to read. Bones that carry only an expression and no influence weight now get a row too; keying rows off weight sites alone had hidden them entirely.
- Two things keep this honest rather than fitted. A block has to be consumed to its final byte and the grammar has no per-block free parameters, so landing exactly on 1,857 block boundaries is not something arbitrary rules do; and `record_count` in the header — the total record count across every block, which nothing in the grammar can influence — is reproduced exactly for `cd_m0001_00_bear`, the one rig whose blocks all decode. The earlier attempt at this was abandoned for producing rules like "a string then ten floats then four bytes", which is not a struct anyone wrote; the difference here is that every rule is a self-validating shape (a counted list, a length-prefixed printable string) rather than a byte tally. The remaining 684 blocks stop at a handful of distinct constructs and are reported as `partial` with the offset they stopped at, never guessed past. Decoding stays strictly read-only: the writer still carries block bytes verbatim, so a misread construct cannot corrupt a file.

### Fixed
- Wired the Driven bones and Rig behaviour tabs to the character the Studio is showing. Both shipped as panels that loaded nothing: they had working loaders, resolvers and export paths, but nothing ever called them, so every visit showed "No constraint rig loaded for this character" and "Pose-modifier descriptor not loaded" no matter which character was on screen. Opening either tab now reads the archives once and shows the current character's rig — the player's `phm_01.papr` at 190 bones in 71 chains, and the 223 of 2,779 pose-modifier settings keyed to `phm_01.pab` — and switching character re-targets both. They key on the session's resolved `.pab`, not on its model id, because a customization variant such as Damian has no skeleton of its own and runs on `phw_01.pab`; keying on the model would find nothing for exactly the characters most likely to be open. Finding either file means walking the package tables at about four seconds a pass, so one pass collects every `.papr` and the descriptor together, is cached for the window, and happens on first open rather than at startup — switching character after that costs nothing. Only the visible tab refreshes; the other catches up when opened.
- Stopped the "What you can do here" box dropping text off its own bottom edge. Word-wrapped `QLabel`s report a one-line `sizeHint`, the box was pinned to that hint by `QSizePolicy.Maximum`, and every bullet long enough to wrap lost its tail — "follows its drivers" and "warning above" both vanished mid-panel, and one entry disappeared entirely. The labels are now short enough not to wrap at all, gated at 30 characters by a test, with each one's reason on its tooltip where it costs no height.
- Made both rig panels legible at the width the Studio actually gives them. The tab column is about 620px and both panels were laid out for more: Driven bones put two tables side by side, leaving the chain list one truncated column wide with Bones, Strength and Decoded off the right edge, and Rig behaviour middle-elided its Setting column into `DefaultData/ArmP...and · LeftHand` while pushing Applies-to off-screen. The tables now stack, Rig behaviour leads each row with the leaf name and elides the path instead, its Note column moved to a tooltip, and both export boxes are one row rather than a form — that alone had been spending 200px of a 780px panel on two text inputs. Each panel opens on a selected row, so the detail view and the value box arrive populated rather than blank with every control greyed out.

### Changed
- Said what the Driven bones tab is for, next to the warning that provokes the question. The banner explains that nothing in the game appears to read `.papr` and left it there; a modder reasonably concluded the tab was pointless. It now says in the same breath that the tab is for reading and experimenting with the shipped rigs, that only 20 characters ship one at all, and that Rig behaviour is where secondary-motion changes actually take effect. The banner's evidence moved to its tooltip to make room. Rig behaviour gained the same kind of opening line, in terms of what the values do — how far the head turns, how much the spine lags, how far limbs reach, how a cart's suspension travels. A character with no `.papr` is now told so by name instead of being shown a message that reads like a broken tool, and a skeleton with no pose-modifier block of its own is told which rig it is looking at instead and that editing it will not change the viewport.

### Fixed
- Stopped the sculpt brushes applying one extra dab when you let go. Releasing the mouse sends a terminal phase whose only job is to close the stroke, but it carried the same brush payload as every other step and the mesh core executes before it closes — so Smooth, Inflate and Pinch sculpted once more at the release point, and a click that never moved got sculpted twice at full strength. Measured against the real core on a 16×16 grid: begin and each drag step moved 576 vertices, and so did the release. Those three tools are sample-driven — Smooth relaxes by weight alone and Inflate and Pinch take their amount from the brush radius — so an unmoved release is not self-cancelling the way a drag-driven tool's is. Grab is left exactly as it was: its displacement comes from the drag itself, so its release is already inert when the pointer has not moved and still applies the last few pixels of travel when it has. The terminator now sends zero strength, which is the one lever all four tools honour, and the release moves 0 vertices while the stroke still closes — verified by opening a second stroke straight after, which the core rejects if the first is still open. Undo continues to treat a stroke as one operation.
- Fixed the viewport's authoritative selection being thrown away on an ordinary click. Selecting a vertex, face or part asks the app what the selection should be and then applies the answer; that answer was being dropped as uncorrelated, leaving the viewport on its own optimistic guess until some later unrelated broadcast happened to refresh it. The gate deciding whether a request may be answered with a selection qualified *every* kind of request by a command name — but only the toolbar commands (Select All, Grow, Invert, Undo, and so on) carry one. A click carries a tool, not a command, so it could never qualify. Verified against the real editor by clicking, answering with a distinctive selection and reading it back: before, the viewport's selection stayed empty; after, it matches what was sent. Strokes stay excluded deliberately — a brush or transform never edits the selection, so its answer only echoes what the viewport already has, and holding a request open for each one would fill the correlation table at pointer-move rate and start evicting the real selections waiting to be rolled back.
- Kept Select All, Invert and Grow inside the geometry you can actually edit. They walked the whole document, which in a comparison session includes the untouched original loaded alongside your replacement, so they offered up vertices and faces no edit can reach; every payload the viewport sends is clamped to the editable range. Edge mode was wrong the same way, since edge topology is built across the whole document. The same commands also scanned the full edge list once per selected element, which is quadratic and freezes the window on a real game mesh rather than merely being slow; they now build the adjacency they need in a single pass.

### Added
- Added a reader, surgical editor and Rig behaviour panel for `character/descriptors/posemodifierdata/posemodifierdata.xml` — 119 KB and 2,779 settings across 98 skeletons holding the rig behaviour the game actually runs: how far a creature turns its head to look at you, how much a spine lags behind a turn, the reach and bending axis of each IK limb, wheel radii and suspension travel on every cart. Unlike `.papr` this one is demonstrably live; the engine's own `pa::engineScript::PoseModifier*` classes are named after its sections. It is deliberately not run through an XML parser: the file is eleven `<PoseModifierDataList>` roots one after another, with nine anonymous `</>` closing tags, a BOM, no declaration, and 24 comments including the Korean labels that are the only human-readable identification a vehicle block has. `ElementTree` refuses it outright, and re-serialising would fix it into strict XML, drop the comments and rewrite the hand-authored tabs — thousands of changed bytes against an engine parser whose tolerances are unknown. So the reader scans, records the character span of every value, and edits by patching spans: the shipped file round-trips byte for byte, and changing one number produces a one-byte length delta and exactly one changed setting. Scaling keeps a value's shape and its number style, so a `-45 57` range stays two numbers and `-60.0` halves to `-30.0` rather than `-30`.
- The panel keys on `.pab` skeleton, which is how the file is organised and how the Studio already identifies a character, so the player shows its 223 applicable settings rather than all 2,779. Two things it refuses to hide: one block commonly serves several characters — the player's LookAt is shared with `phw_01`, `ptm_01` and `pdem_01` — so the Applies-to column names them, because that is a consequence the file does not show; and each section can carry a `DisabledKeyList`, where an edit does nothing and explains nothing, so a banner says so. A skeleton that appears *only* in a disabled list is still selectable, since that is exactly the character whose owner needs telling — the first version keyed the picker off settings alone and made those creatures unreachable.

### Added
- Validated the prefab path rewriter against the game's own authoring tool, which is the first check on this work that does not rest on our own decoder. The archives ship thousands of prefabs that are the same asset with a single path changed — armour variants, weapon indexes — so those pairs are ground truth: rewrite vanilla A to point at B's path and the result must be byte identical to vanilla B. **15,742 of 15,750 pairs reproduce exactly, including 10,066 of 10,066 length-changing ones**, which are the cases that exercise pointer relocation, pointee length fields and the data header. The other 8 are refused as undecidable, and nothing fails. Choosing genuine pairs is most of the work and getting it wrong manufactures both false failures and false confidence: pairs must agree on the full type table including members, on every decoded object and numeric value, on all strings but one, on a file-size difference equal to that string's byte delta, and on every remaining byte once the fields a shift legitimately moves are masked. Without that last filter, `_index01` versus `_index02` variants qualify and then "fail" because the rewrite faithfully preserves A's index. `scripts/prefab_vanilla_pair_oracle.py` runs it against any extracted corpus and exits non-zero on any failure; it is mutation tested, and an earlier version of it that excused residual differences matching A was discarded after that rule hid a broken data-header patch entirely.

### Fixed
- Made the path rewriter read its own output back before returning it, and refuse it if anything is off. The checks — the file still decodes completely, every string reads back as expected, the pointer count is unchanged, the declared size matches the real one, and the size change matches what the edits imply — already existed, but only inside the fuzz harness. A harness proves the cases it runs; this refuses the case in front of the user. Every one of the 63 corruptions an earlier length-field bug produced re-decoded as garbage, so this would have stopped all of them before a mod was written. It says nothing about whether the game accepts a file; it converts an undiscovered structural mistake from "export a broken mod" into "refuse to write one". Verified to reject nothing valid: the vanilla-pair oracle is unchanged at 10,124 of 10,124 length-changing rewrites, and the corpus fuzz at zero corruptions.
- The first version of that check was itself unsound, and the mutation test caught it. It located the expected strings with the writer's own shift table, so it agreed with the writer even when the writer was wrong — zeroing that table produced a broken file the check passed happily. It now compares the decoded strings as an ordered sequence, which is a property of the result alone and shares no arithmetic with the rewrite. A guard test asserts it never calls the shift helper again.

### Added
- Fuzzed the in-place value writer, which was the one write path the randomised testing did not cover. Path rewrites move bytes and had been fuzzed since; placements and plain values are written in place and only had example-based tests. Over the shipped archives, 501 prefabs carrying editable values took a full batch write each: every file the same size afterwards, still decoding, with exactly the requested bytes at the requested offsets and everything outside the written spans untouched. Committed as seeded synthetic cases plus a guard that a stale expected-old value is refused for plain values and not only for placements.

### Docs
- Re-ran the search for a byte identifying a marker=1 group's component type, on **5,003 anchors** rather than the original 375 — the trailer fix having produced far more completed walks to anchor on. The best position now scores 14.9% against the earlier 4.0%, which looks like progress and is not: the resolved index and that byte both cluster on small values, and the agreement expected from that overlap alone is 11.8%. A 3-point excess is not a discriminator, and the apparent jump is a change in the anchor set's composition. Recorded with the independence baseline so the next reader compares against the right number.
- Recorded that retrying the alternative component on a binary choice is exponential after all. The reasoning was that trying one alternative at one group cannot cascade; it does, because nested groups each retry in turn and the cost multiplies down the tree. A corpus pass that normally takes about a minute had not finished in ten. Reverted.

### Docs
- Checked the prefab edit JSON path, which predates this work and had never been opened while the edit APIs were rebuilt around it. The round trip is intact: 400 of 400 corpus prefabs export and re-import byte-identically. It does still reach `crimson_formats.rebuild_prefab_resized_strings` — the scanner that finds pointers by looking for u32s which happen to equal a known string offset — for length-changing replacements, and I rerouted that through the exact rewriter before reverting it. The reroute was wrong: the flag is unreachable from the UI, with two guard tests keeping it that way, and its only caller is a corpus probe whose entire purpose is to measure that scanner's behaviour, so replacing the writer silently changed what the instrument measured and moved a golden. The route is recorded here as flag-gated, guarded and deliberately left alone rather than quietly re-pointed.

### Added
- Answered "where else is this used?" from the row menu. A modder retargeting one armour piece had no way to tell whether that edit covered the set or was one of twenty, and the answer decides whether the mod works. The lookup reads every prefab in the archive, rejecting almost all of them on a plain substring test before anything is decoded — 12,000 prefabs in under a second on loose data — and confirms through the pointer records rather than the walk, so files the walk cannot finish still answer correctly. Archive access stays with the browser rather than the modal, which is what keeps a dialog from reading archives on the UI thread.
- Gave the rows recovered past a walk stop their file's own name in the first column. They were blank there, because no member name exists for them — the walk never reached them, so nothing says which field they belong to, and inventing one would be exactly the plausible lie the rest of the dialog avoids. The basename identifies the file and a column of those is scannable where a column of full paths is not.

### Changed
- Split `prefab_recovery.py` out of the decoder and the editor tests out of the dialog tests, both because the size ratchet caught them and raising the cap was not the answer. Reading a prefab *without* walking it is a job worth naming: the walk is the richer reading but stops on 38.1% of files, and everything in the new module works from pointer records instead, which is what lets a partly-read prefab list its files, accept the retargets that move nothing, and answer what else references a path.

### Docs
- Established that the prefabs which do not decode are **one problem, not eight**. The census listed eight distinct failure messages; completion turns out to track a single variable, which is how many candidate component types a file declares. With one candidate the walk completes 99.5% of the time. With two it drops to 44.7%, with three to 9.5%, and with eleven or more to about 6%. Files that fail early declare 18.8 types and 12.7 candidates on average against 5.3 and 1.6 for files that complete. The walk is essentially perfect when it has nothing to choose between, and fails as soon as it must choose — because marker=1 groups state no component type and the declaration-order fallback is a guess that is nearly free with one candidate and nearly hopeless with twelve. That explains why the messages differ while the shape does not: a wrong component means a wrong member layout, the cursor desynchronises, and whichever check trips first supplies the wording. It also explains why none of the individual causes yielded to a targeted fix and why the mask-width variants could not help. Any real gain has to come from a discriminator for marker=1 groups; nothing downstream of that choice will do it.
- Replaced the standing "the pointer test could in principle be wrong" caveat with a measurement. `value == offset + 4` cannot be proved sufficient, but it can be checked against the walk, which knows exactly which pointers it consumed: across 1,261 fully-read prefabs and **18,503 pointer sites** the two agree exactly on 99.0% of files, and 35 of the 37 sites found but never traversed open with the zero word a populated pointee begins with — which is what an unvisited pointer looks like, not a coincidence. One site went the other way and the harness can manufacture that, so it is not claimed as a genuine miss.

### Changed
- Split the row building out of the Prefab Inspector dialog. The work above pushed it to 1,021 lines and the size ratchet caught it, which is the guard doing its job. Rather than raise the cap, `prefab_inspector_rows.py` now owns turning a decoded prefab into rows — which objects become headings, what each row is called, which rows can be changed, and what to show for a file the walk could not finish. That is a nameable job rather than a slice taken to satisfy a counter, which is the rule the guard's own docstring sets.

### Fixed
- Stopped offering swaps that cannot be written. Allowing same-length retargets on partly-read prefabs left the file picker still listing every candidate, so a modder would choose one, work through the review, and be refused at the last step for a constraint they were never shown. The picker now offers only names of exactly the same byte length when that is all that can be written, and says why the list is short.

### Changed
- Made the walk's collection error report what is actually at the cursor. "collection count 1867710464 (kind 98)" reads as an unhandled kind byte and sends the next reader off to enumerate kinds — an enumeration that does not exist, since the rejected kinds are scattered and the real fault is that the cursor is misaligned. It now says what it found instead: a length-prefixed string and its text, an owner field, or the raw bytes, and by how many bytes the real record starts further on where that can be determined.

### Docs
- Re-tested the presence mask's width, because the earlier dead end was measured at a 54.3% baseline and the trailer fix moved that to 61.9% — a dead end measured against a stale baseline is not settled. Every narrower reading is still worse: low byte only scores 31.46%, `u8` for marker=1 scores 61.41%, `u8` for marker<=2 scores 58.89%, against 61.88% for the full `u16`. That closes the lead the mask-exceeds investigation pointed at.
- Recorded that reading more prefabs made the undecidable-pointee refusals slightly more common, not less: the files the trailer fix unlocked carry proportionally more name-record pointers, so exact knowledge of pointee length fields fell from 50.5% to 47.8% of pointer sites and ambiguity rose from 6.5% to 8.9%. Those edits are declined rather than guessed, and the files could not be edited at all before. Deriving the name record's extent would resolve it and cannot be done cheaply — the distance from the name text to the length field is scattered across 86, 108, 112, 132 and 242 bytes, because the pointee encloses the whole object.

### Docs
- Chased the three next-largest walk failures to a conclusion and recorded that none of them is what its message says. "Collection count N (kind N)" is not an unhandled kind: the rejected kind bytes are scattered and the bytes at the cursor are length-prefixed strings, so the walk is misaligned and reading a string as a collection header — the message invites an enumeration that does not exist. "Walk ended N bytes short" is genuinely unconsumed structure, with owner fields and object names in the leftovers, but only 37 of 729 parse as further groups when the walk resumes from the stop, so resume-parsing was tried and dropped. "Mask exceeds every candidate" fails on the first *child* group, never the root, and the child's mask needs more members than any candidate has while the root type usually would fit — but offering the root as a peer is actively worse (61.9% down to 59.4%, since it fits most masks and displaces the right component) and offering it as a last resort gains six files while relocating the failure to the same 1% progress. Both results point at the mask's width being misread rather than at the candidate list. Recorded in the decode doc so the next attempt starts from the evidence instead of the error text.

### Fixed
- Read 904 more prefabs, by learning that the blob's trailer is a run of records rather than one. The failure census pointed straight at this: 1,036 files — 18.9% of every incomplete walk — stopped with "no element header" at a *median 99%* of the way through, which is not a structural gap, it is something at the very end. Comparing the last bytes of files that do read against what is left over on files that do not showed the same five-byte `01 <u32>` record in both, just more of it: a completed blob ends `.. 00 00 00 01`, and the trailer rule accepted a fixed 5 or 6 bytes, so a file with two of those records had one too many. The rule now accepts a run of them, and a collection whose declared count outruns the data ends rather than breaks when only that run remains. Completion goes from 54.4% to **61.9%**. Both harnesses confirm it on more data than before rather than less: the vanilla-pair oracle now finds 15,872 usable pairs and reproduces 10,124 of 10,124 length-changing rewrites byte-for-byte with zero failures, and the corpus fuzz covers 1,541 files with zero corruptions.

### Added
- Said what a model swap really changes. A prefab points at a mesh and nothing else, but the engine resolves that mesh's material and physics from its path — so retargeting silently swaps those too, and the tool only mentioned it in a log line, one companion of the two. The review step now names every companion the new model will pull in and whether it exists, and states that they come from the game's own files rather than the package. They are deliberately *not* bundled: the new mesh's companions already ship with the game, so copying them in would add mod conflicts and change nothing, and a companion that is genuinely missing is already a blocking warning. That reasoning is now a comment in the export path so it does not get "fixed" later.
- Made a partly-read prefab say where it stopped. It said the rest "uses a structure this tool cannot follow yet", which is not something a modder can report or act on and not something the next person to work on decoding can use either. The decoder now records the byte offset where the walk gave up, and the banner says how far through the data section it got and what stopped it — "got 72% of the way through and stopped at byte 3,323 (walk ended 210 bytes short)".
- Let modders retarget a partly-read prefab, as far as it is provably safe to. Editing was refused outright whenever the walk could not finish, which is 45.6% of shipped prefabs — but that refusal was about relocating bytes, and a replacement of exactly the same byte length relocates nothing: no pointer moves, no pointee length changes, no header size is touched, and every byte outside the replaced text keeps its offset. That needs no structure at all, only pointer records that can be read, which is what the recovery above establishes. Verified by applying one to all 669 partly-read prefabs in a sample that carry a recoverable asset path: 669 applied, every file the same size afterwards, the new name present, and no file's readability changed. Anything that would resize is still refused, and now says why and by how much. The banner stops saying saving is switched off and says what is available instead.
- Made a partly-read prefab list the files it actually uses. The structural walk stops at the first thing it cannot follow, and 45.6% of shipped prefabs stop somewhere — those showed only the rows read before the stop, which reads as "this prefab barely references anything" rather than "this tool gave up early". Pointer records are found by the exact identity test, which does not depend on the walk reaching them, and a populated pointee is a zero word followed by a length-prefixed string, so the reference list can be recovered directly. Checked against 635 fully-read prefabs, that recovers every resource the walk found, at identical offsets and identical text, and nineteen strings besides — so it never contradicts the walk where both apply. Across the 5,478 partly-read prefabs it surfaces **87,493** asset paths the walk missed, in 3,027 files, one of them gaining 9,263 on its own. They appear under "Files found past the stop", with a tooltip saying they came from the file's pointer records rather than from reading its structure.

### Added
- Made the big prefabs usable. The "What this prefab contains" tab had no filter, which is fine for the median prefab — one object and two rows — but the tail runs to 1,126 objects and 2,252 rows, and 87 of the 6,522 readable prefabs carry more than 50 objects. Those were unusable. There is now a filter box matching name, field, value or path, and an "Only rows I can change" tick that hides everything read-only. A row is meaningless without knowing which object owns it, so a matching row keeps its object on screen, and a match on an object's own name keeps all of its rows. A count under the tree says how many rows survived, or that nothing matched.

### Added
- Widened the field glossary from the fields prefabs *set* to the fields they *declare*, which is what the "Fields explained" tab actually shows. Set-field coverage was already 99.8% — only five uncovered names across 1,384 files, all rare — but declared coverage was 85 names of 755, or 85.5% of occurrences, and the gap was one big cluster: spline points and Houdini authoring data, declared on nearly every world prefab and shown as de-camel-cased names with nothing said about them. 30 entries later that is 115 names and 91.2%. Three of them match the game's own misspellings exactly — `_outterTangent`, `_lenghtToNextPoint`, `_smootingMaxCount` — and say so in the description, because these are lookup keys and correcting the spelling silently loses the entry. `_hdaFileName` is named as a Houdini Digital Asset and flagged as authoring data the game does not load, so a modder can tell build inputs from runtime assets. Fields whose names do not support a confident reading get a label and no claim, as before.

### Added
- Added per-row undo to the Prefab Inspector. "Undo all changes" was the only way back, so one mistyped path cost every other edit in the session — which quietly pushes people towards keeping a bad edit rather than losing eight good ones. Right-click a changed row and "Undo this row" puts that one back and leaves the rest alone. Undoing a transform also undoes its twin, since leaving one of the pair edited would recreate exactly the `_worldTransform`/`_tiledTransform` mismatch the pairing exists to prevent.

### Fixed
- Closed a second, unsafe prefab writer that a checkbox could reach. The attachment placement dialog offered "Experimental: allow length-changing prefab role/socket patch", whose own proof line admitted "binary offset/fixup safety is not proven" — and it was not: it spliced each new length-prefixed socket name over the old span and stopped there, leaving every absolute pointer after the edit addressing the wrong byte, no pointee length field corrected and the data header's size stale. That is precisely the corruption class the exact rewriter exists to prevent, sitting one tick away in the UI. It now routes through `rewrite_prefab_paths`, which relocates all of it and reproduces the game's own output on 10,066 of 10,066 length-changing prefabs in the archives. The trade is honest: a prefab the decoder cannot read all the way through is now refused with a reason rather than spliced, and the message says same-length replacements still work. Same-length patches are untouched. The checkbox says what it does instead of calling itself experimental.

### Added
- Told modders how to get back. Every generated package explained how to install itself and nothing explained how to remove it — the step someone needs exactly when the game has stopped working and is least able to go looking for. Package readmes now carry a "Removing This Mod" section: disable it in the manager, delete the folder, and that is the whole of the fix, because the original game files were never modified. The Prefab Inspector's export prompt says the same thing and adds the part only it knows — prefab editing is new and has not yet been confirmed to load in game, so a modder can tell which of their mods is the experimental one before installing rather than after something breaks.
- Made a prefab's ordinary settings editable, not just its file paths and placements. Flags, distances and counts were decoded and shown all along but read-only, which covered 72.9% of readable prefabs — 980 float members and 171 `uint8` ones across the sample — including things a modder actually wants, like `_shrinkMaskDistance`. Each row now offers an editor matched to the member's declared type: a tick for a bool, a whole-number box for an integer, three boxes for a `float3`. Only members whose declared type and byte width agree are offered at all, because writing a value whose type is unconfirmed is how a readable file becomes a broken one, and a number outside its type's range is refused with a reason rather than truncated. Writes are in place and fixed-size, so nothing moves and no pointer needs relocating.
- Added a review step before a prefab edit is built, so a modder sees the whole change in one place instead of a sentence in a status line. It lists every pending change with the label the row showed, the value now, the value after, and anything that follows from it — including how many other rows still point at the old file, since edits are addressed by occurrence and the copies deliberately stay put, which is the opposite of what most people expect. Warnings now block: a role mismatch or a target that does not exist in the archives disables the confirm button until the modder ticks "Save anyway - I have read the warnings above". Previously they were advisory text nothing stopped you scrolling past, which meant the tool had effectively decided not to act on its own checks. Cancelling writes nothing and says so.

### Fixed
- Fixed an animation swap running even when the move it was meant to accompany had failed. Both failure paths in `_apply_carry_move` — a row with no descriptor entry, and `set_route` raising — returned silently, and the caller went on to replace the clips and report success, producing a mod that plays a back draw for a weapon still on the hip. It now reports which failure occurred and the swap is abandoned.
- Fixed the Studio being able to abort on close. `_stop_swap` called `wait(3000)` and discarded the result, clearing its references whether or not the thread had stopped; closing the window while a clip read was stalled left Qt collecting a running `QThread`. It now only releases the worker once it has actually finished.
- Fixed a partly applied animation swap reporting as a complete one. Clips the worker could not read never reach the payload and `replace_clip` failures were skipped, yet the message counted only successes. It now reports what could not be read or written, and says so plainly when the clip it offers to play was itself skipped rather than claiming it was replaced.
- Fixed the move dialog swallowing a move after the item was changed. `plan()` compared the destination against the socket of whichever row was selected when the dialog opened, so choosing a different item and moving it to its own current position silently did nothing. The "hangs on now" line now follows the selected row.

- Established what a prefab's placement numbers are actually measured against, and stopped the editor asserting one answer for every field. It said "Scale and position are in world units" regardless of which member was open. Measured across the archives: `_worldTransform` and `_tiledTransform` appear only on world objects — 5,228 of each, median absolute position component 61 units with 81.6% above 10 — so world is right for those; `_offsetTransform` is named for what it is and is not; and character prefabs carry no transforms at all. Each field now says what its position is measured from, and an unrecognised one says the basis is not established rather than defaulting to world, because naming the wrong basis makes every nudge wrong.
- Kept a prefab's two transforms in step. Objects carry `_worldTransform` and `_tiledTransform` as a pair, and in all 5,228 shipped objects that have both, the two hold the same scale, rotation and position — 100%, with the tile index always zero. Moving one and leaving the other produced a combination the game's own data never contains, and it was invisible: the tree collapses the duplicate to "same as ...", so there was no second row to correct by hand. Editing either now moves its identical twin to match, preserving each copy's own tile index, and says so in the log.

### Changed
- Cut the Studio viewport's mesh draw from 9.8 ms to 3.8 ms a frame, taking a playing frame with solid shading from 12.8 ms to 9.5 ms (about 105 fps), and from 14.8 ms to 11.0 ms at armour-scale geometry. Two changes: the depth-sorted face list now holds plain coordinates instead of a `QPolygonF` per triangle, with the path built directly by `moveTo`/`lineTo` in the batching loop; and the outline pen is dropped in favour of `fillPath` while anything is moving. That pen existed only to hide an antialiasing artefact — with `NoPen`, AA leaves a sub-pixel gap along every shared edge — and antialiasing is already disabled during motion, so it was pure cost exactly when it mattered. Measured in isolation the two are worth about 2.5 ms; the rest comes from no longer allocating ~1,500 Qt objects a frame and sorting tuples that hold them.

- Took half the guesswork out of prefab editing by having the decoder remember what it already read. Each pointee ends in a length field recording its own byte count, and the editor located it by scanning for a u32 equal to its distance from the pointee start — the search that landed on a nested string's length prefix and corrupted 63 of 1,371 shipped files. But the walk *consumes and validates* that field as it decodes, so its position was known all along and simply thrown away. The decoder now records it, and the rewriter uses the recorded position for the 50.5% of pointer sites the walk routes through. The remainder are name-record pointers, whose pointee has a different shape; those still take the masked scan, and are still refused when ambiguous. Undecidable refusals across the corpus drop from 59 to 52, byte-exact involutions rise to 1,319, corruptions stay at zero, and the vanilla-pair oracle is unchanged at 10,066 of 10,066 length-changing rewrites reproduced exactly.

### Fixed
- Fixed a corrupting bug in the prefab path rewriter, found by fuzzing it against the shipped archives. A pointee's trailing length field was located by scanning for the first position whose u32 equals its own distance from the pointee start. That test is necessary but not sufficient: pointees nest, so the scan crosses string data, and a string's own u32 length prefix satisfies it whenever the string sits `len(text)` bytes into the pointee. The scan stopped there — patching the *string's* length prefix and leaving the real length field stale. On 1,371 shipped prefabs, random multi-edits corrupted **63 of them**, every one re-decoding into garbage. The bug had been present since the feature shipped and the original 1,500 round-trips never saw it, because they edited one path at a time in files where the coincidence does not arise. Two changes fix it: positions inside decoded string data are no longer offered, and where a pointee opens with a decoded string its field is *computed* rather than searched, which covers every resource-path pointee. What remains genuinely undecidable — 6.5% of pointees have two surviving candidates and a nesting rule resolved 0 of 244 — is now refused rather than guessed at, because a pointee's length only needs rewriting when an edit falls inside it. The corpus run is now **0 corruptions**, with about 4% of files declining the edit instead.
- Told modders that "Save changes" does not save. It built the edited file, logged "Applied 1 path change(s)", and stopped — the destination is only chosen after the window closes, and the tooltip ("Write the changes into a copy") claimed a write that had not happened. The button is now "Save changes..." and the log says plainly that nothing is on disk yet and what happens next.

### Changed
- Established what the 8 bytes at offset 6 of a version-4 prefab are, and it is not what the decoder called them. Labelling them a content hash raised a real worry, because neither rewriter touches the field: if the engine validated it, every edited v4 file would be rejected in game, and that would explain a failed test mod with nothing else wrong. It cannot be that. The field is two independent u32s rather than one value, and six byte-identical bodies in the corpus carry different values there — so nothing about the content determines it. No hash reproduces either half: crc32, adler32, FNV-1a and FNV-1 32-bit, djb2, sdbm and md5 tried over the whole file with the field removed, with it zeroed, everything after it, the blob alone and the schema alone, plus the archive path and stem as strings; zero matches out of all of them. It is not a cross-reference either — none of 1,467 sampled values appears inside any prefab body. Near-unique at 11,654 distinct across 11,996 files and clustering loosely by asset folder, it reads as an authoring-time identifier. Preserving it verbatim, which both rewriters already did, is correct, and a test now locks that in so nobody "fixes" it into a recomputed checksum.

### Fixed
- Made the prefab edit APIs mean what they said. Path replacements were keyed by the old path text, so editing one row rewrote *every* occurrence of that path in the file, and two rows sharing a path collapsed through a dictionary with the last one winning. `plan_prefab_path_edits` now also takes a sequence of `PrefabPathEdit`, addressed by byte offset and checked against the string that actually decoded there, and the inspector uses that form — the mapping form stays, documented as all-occurrences, because that is the right default for retargeting a mesh everywhere. Separately, `rewrite_prefab_placements` promised that "an offset from a stale decode is refused", but it decoded the very payload it then compared against, so the comparison could never fail. It now takes `(expected_old, new)` per offset — only the caller knows what it read, so only the caller can say what it expects to still be there — plus an optional `source_digest` that rejects the whole batch if the file changed underneath. The bare-bytes form still works and is documented as unverified.
- Stopped the Prefab Inspector telling modders that guessed data is accurate. Where a prefab does not state an object's component type the walk infers it from declaration order, and an inferred object decodes just as tidily as a correct one — but the banner said "Everything shown is accurate" regardless, and for partial reads that claim was flatly untrue. Objects now carry whether their type was `stated` or `inferred`, measured across the corpus at 29,841 stated against 376 inferred, so 1.2% of objects in 4.7% of fully-read files are guesses. Those rows now say "best guess, the file does not say what this is" in the row itself rather than only in a banner — a header cannot tell you which of eight objects is the uncertain one — and the summary counts them. The element-header reader was also hardened: with marker 1 the byte at `owner-3` is the mask's own high byte, so reading it as a type index was reading the mask twice. It never got through the member-count check on any of the 376 marker-1 groups, but that was luck rather than design, and it is now refused outright. Confirmed a no-op: 6,522 completed walks before and after.
- Stopped the Prefab Inspector's placement editor rewriting a rotation nobody touched. It seeded its boxes from `rotation_degrees`, which is a display rendering rounded to two decimals and documents itself as read-only, then rebuilt the quaternion unconditionally on OK — so nudging a position by a centimetre wrote a different rotation. Measured across the shipped archives: 3,445 of 4,321 placements came back with different rotation bytes, 0.60% of unit quaternions drifted more than a degree, and the worst case was a full **90 degrees**. That worst case is not exotic — it is every weapon child socket, which sits at pitch 90 where yaw and roll turn about the same axis and Euler angles stop being separable. Each of the three groups is now written back only if one of its boxes actually moved; an untouched group reuses the decoded value verbatim, so opening a transform and clicking OK is byte-identical. When rotation *is* edited the boxes seed at six decimals instead of two, and a pole orientation now says so on screen rather than silently mangling the result.

### Changed
- Cut the per-file size guard from 280 caps to 101 and made it say what it is for. It was being read as the project's line limit, which it never was: `tests/test_architecture_size_ratchets.py` already applies 1,000 lines per file and 150 per function to every owned Python, native and C# file, grandfathering today's offenders in `architecture_size_baseline*.json` — all 71 `cdmw/` files over 1,000 lines are covered there. So `test_architecture_file_sizes.py` is only the tighter-than-default layer, for files that must stay small, and 179 of its entries were adding nothing on top of the ratchet: 168 restated the default, and 11 were fossils left behind when a file was split, the worst capping a 109-line file at 7,545 and a 26-line file at 1,800. Those are gone. The 10 caps that sat exactly at their file's current length are gone too — a cap with no headroom turns an added comment into a build failure, which teaches people to delete comments instead of thinking about structure, so every kept cap now carries at least ten lines or five percent. Two new checks stop the table rotting the same way again: a cap at or above the default is rejected as redundant, and an entry naming a file that no longer exists is rejected as stale. Failures are now collected and reported together rather than one `assert` at a time, so a size sweep takes one run instead of one run per file. The guard also reads through a repo root derived from `__file__` rather than resolving paths against the working directory, so it passes from anywhere. Written into the module docstring, because the count cannot express it: never split a file solely to satisfy the counter — a split has to leave two parts you can name, and `..._state_a` / `..._state_b` is what it looks like when that rule is ignored.
- Found that the game very likely does not read `.papr` at all, and made the Driven bones panel say so before anything else. Searching the shipped binaries for the format's schema turned up something more useful: `papr` appears zero times as a standalone token across all 40+ executables and DLLs in `bin64/`, where `pac` appears 8 times, `pab` 6, `paseq` 4, and `pamlod`, `meshinfo` and `pathc` twice each. The vocabulary *inside* the files — `Local_Euler`, `ExposeTransform` — appears in no binary in either ASCII or UTF-16, and 1,015 character descriptor and model files reference `papr` nowhere. The contents are 3ds Max constructs: Expose Transform helpers and MAXScript controller expressions such as `amin(((Local_Euler_Y-(degToRad 63.748))*0.7)+1.0) 0`. Twenty files ship, against 316,059 `.paa`. The reading that fits all of it is an authoring artifact left in the archives with no runtime behind it, so an edit is very unlikely to do anything in game. That is evidence rather than proof — a reference could exist in data outside the files scanned — but it is what a modder needs to know before spending an evening tuning numbers, so the panel leads with it and a test asserts the warning is on screen. `.papr` drops to low priority in the manifest.
- Recorded the two files that *do* have a runtime behind them, both plain XML already in the archives and needing no decoding: `character/descriptors/jiggledescriptor.xml` carries the real jiggle knobs (`LinearDamping`, `AngularDamping`, and a per-bone `MaskBone` list for `bear` and `Wolf`, naming the same `B_Jiggle_*` bones `.papr` defines), and `character/descriptors/posemodifierdata/posemodifierdata.xml` is 119 KB of runtime pose-modifier data keyed by `.pab`, covering Vehicle, AimIK, RootBoneIK, SpineTrain, LimbIK, LookAt, Multileg, FishingRod, BoneAim and Harness — wheel radii, suspension lengths, yaw and pitch limits and damping coefficients, all as text attributes. The engine's own vocabulary for the system is `pa::engineScript::PoseModifier*` in the RTTI names, which is what ties those two files to the runtime.

### Added
- Cracked the `.papr` entry structure and gave constraint rigs a real writer. The obvious approach — find each block's closing `07 05 00` — cannot work: those bytes also occur inside float payloads and inside the expression strings some rigs carry (`ExposeTransform_Bip01 R Forearm:5`), so the first match is often too early and the walk desyncs. What works is locating entry *starts* instead — two name-shaped strings followed by a tail whose third byte is 0 or 1 — and requiring that chain to be exactly `entry_count` long and to tile the file. 19 of the 20 shipped rigs tile exactly; the 20th finds 236 starts against a declared 237 and is rejected rather than guessed at. Two more pieces fell out: the third tail byte selects a 40-byte transform frame (`scale[3]`, `rotation[4]`, `translation[3]` — the same shape as the `.paa` bounds frame, 703 of them across the corpus and previously unread), and the `u32` at `0x20` is the total tag-record count across all blocks, which is an independent check on any future attempt at the block grammar. All 19 parsed rigs now rebuild byte for byte across 2,734 bones, 703 transform frames and 1,632 weights, and bone names may be renamed to a different length because nothing in the format is offset-addressed. Block contents remain deliberately opaque — the per-entry tag stream is schema-driven and the schema is not in the file — so they are carried verbatim and an edit can never corrupt a construct that is not understood.
- Established what a `.papr` actually governs, which is not what its bone names suggest. Across all 471 chains in the twenty shipped rigs, 259 are corrective deformation (`UpperFMuscle`, `Bip01 L Knee_Sub`, `Thigh_Front`, twist bones), 67 are pivots, 56 are exposed transforms, 29 are mechanical parts on the golems and tanks, and only 5 are jiggle — on the dog and the bear. No rig contains hair or cloth at all. So on a player character this file is the deformation rig: how a muscle bulges and how a knee creases as the body moves, which is what a physique or body mod needs to touch. The first pass at this generalised from those five `B_Jiggle_*` chains and described the format as hair physics; that was wrong and is corrected throughout.
- Decoded one complete `.papr` block shape: 682 of 2,541 config blocks are exactly nine 3-byte records (`05 03 00`, three `10 01 xx`, `06 04 00`, three `10 01 xx`, `07 05 00`) with every byte accounted for. Also pinned down that `0a 04 00` always carries exactly two bytes across all 1,084 occurrences, and that a driver list is `u8 count`, that many `(u16 name, f32 weight)` pairs, then a `0x00` sentinel. The other 1,859 blocks stay opaque: a type-04 member's payload depends on its member id, that schema is not in the file, and fitting the remaining shapes against the corpus produces rules that consume 73% of block bytes and are plainly overfitted (a `11 01` of "string then ten floats then four bytes" is not a struct anyone wrote), so none of that guesswork ships. `u32@0x20` is recorded as the record-count oracle for whoever attacks it next.
- Added a Driven bones panel to the Placement & Animation Studio. It groups the rig into chains rather than listing bones — `golem_imp_boss`'s 437 entries become 13 rows, `phm_01`'s 190 become 71 — and each row says what it is (`deformation`, `jiggle`, `pivot`, `expose`, `mechanical`) so nobody has to infer from bone names that `Bip01 L UpperFMuscle` is a muscle bulge. A Decoded column marks whether every byte of that chain's config is understood; editing is equally safe either way because undecoded bytes are written back unchanged, but the distinction is the modder's to know. Softer/Stiffer/Off/Reset come before the strength slider because that is how people think about it. Scaling rounds to whole percent, because a whole number in 1..100 is what makes a weight locatable at all and halving 15 to 7.5 would leave a value the next pass no longer offers. A "What you can do here" box is generated from a capability list in the code, so the UI cannot promise something the code will not do — a test asserts every "can" has a function behind it and that adding a chain is listed as impossible. Export writes one package per manager through the Studio's existing packager and is disabled until something changes. The panel states plainly that it cannot preview the result: the game solves these bones at runtime, so there is no honest way to show the change in the viewport.

### Fixed
- Fixed the `Move a weapon` dialog appearing to do nothing. `QDialog.Accepted` is a class constant and the handler read it off the dialog instance, which raises — so pressing "Move it" skipped both the routing and the animation swap. Under `pythonw` there is no console, so the exception went nowhere and the button was simply silent. The dialog had tests and the swap had tests; nothing exercised the handler that joins them, which is now covered.
- Fixed a weapon still hanging upside down on the back even after the orientation fix. The stand-in angle is found with `conventional_child_socket`, which reads the descriptor rows *as resolved for the selected weapon* — so with a one-hand sword in hand nothing in view pairs `Spine2_B_MainWeapon_Socket` with a child socket, the lookup came back empty, and the move kept the hip's identity rotation while reporting success. There is now a known pairing per body socket to fall back on, and when the stand-in cannot be applied at all the status line says the item is still aimed by a socket belonging to another part of the body, rather than leaving it to the eye. Reproduced from the report by disabling the borrow, which renders exactly the screenshot that was sent.
- Replaced the scattered move workflow with one `Move a weapon` dialog. It asks the question in the order it is actually asked — which item, from where to where, do the animations come along, at what scope — and lists every animation file with what will replace it, each one tickable, so "which animations" is answered by looking rather than by trusting. Nothing is applied until OK, and an edit that would change nothing cannot be confirmed.
- `crimson_formats.rebuild_prefab_resized_strings` now verifies its own output and refuses rather than returning a corrupted payload. It relocates by scanning preserved bytes for u32s that happen to equal a known string offset, which cannot tell a pointer from a coincidence; on a real prefab that silently dropped an internal pointer. The rebuild is now checked against the exact pointer test and fails loudly, pointing at `prefab_binary_edit.rewrite_prefab_paths`. Payloads that are not prefabs are left alone, since there is nothing to check them against.

### Docs
- Corrected `docs/features/prefab-json-import.md`, which still said length-changing import was gated off, and noted that its "no-edit rebuild" and "layout" checks pass by construction: the layout splits a payload into recovered strings plus opaque preserved spans, so copying both back is byte-identical whether or not the format is understood.
- Added `docs/features/prefab-structural-decoding.md` covering the grammar, why length-changing edits are safe, measured coverage, and the known limits -- including that no edited prefab has yet been confirmed to load in the game.

### Added
- Added a `.paa` writer (`tools/paa_motion/encode.py`), so animation clips can be authored rather than only read. The gate is that a clip written back unedited reproduces its source byte for byte: that holds on 12,000 clips sampled from the archives across 45 distinct flag words and on all 188 in the extracted tree, with no failures. Three things make it exact. The value formats are lossless in this direction — a half read into a float converts back to the same half, and packed components are `signed_byte / 64`, a power of two, so the division is exact. The header's `key_bytes` total is recomputed from the tracks rather than copied, so an edited clip gets a header that agrees with it. And what the reader does not model, it now carries: the bounds frames, the packed codec's two lead words, the table filler and the trailing pad travel on the parse and are written back verbatim. Building the writer forced two findings the reader had been getting away with. Flag `0x04` is not inert — it adds a second 40-byte bounds frame, and reading a fixed 80 bytes put `duration` 40 bytes late on the 18 `aim_add` bow clips that clear it; with the bit honoured they report exactly `31/30` s and every one lands on a whole frame count. And the two bytes before each float translation key are not padding: they are constant within a track, differ between tracks, match no 16 bits of the bone hash, and the same bone carries different values in different files — an uninitialised stack slot, kept per track so a rebuild can put it back.
- Added a `.paloc` reader and writer (`cdmw/core/paloc_format.py`), which is every line of text in the game: 14 language tables, 187,521 entries each. The detail that had kept it closed is that the record count is a *footer*, not a header, so a reader that opens the file looking for one finds a record immediately. All 14 shipped tables — 2,625,294 records — rebuild byte for byte, and the footer count agrees with the record walk in every one. Nothing in the format is offset-addressed or aligned, so a translated line may be any length and rewriting the table is just re-emitting the records: this is the one game format where an edit cannot corrupt anything downstream. `replace_text` is the whole of what a translation mod needs.
- Added a `.papr` reader and in-place weight editor (`cdmw/core/papr_format.py`) for the twenty constraint rigs that drive hair, cloth, tassels and the `B_Jiggle_*` chains. The per-entry record is a typed opcode stream that is *not* solved — one rig walks on the simplest reading and the other nineteen do not — so there is deliberately no `encode_papr`; a writer on a guessed grammar would emit files that load and then misbehave. What is solid is the part that matters: the driver lists are a bone name followed by an `f32` influence weight, and across the twenty rigs that locates 1,774 of them, every one a whole number between 1 and 100 and 98.6% multiples of five. Editing one is a four-byte overwrite, a no-op edit is byte-identical on all twenty, and `set_weights` refuses to write unless the caller states the value it expects to replace.
